<meta>This page continues the mathematical exercises from Bhāskara II’s Līlāvatī, transitioning from the "broken necklace" problem on the previous page to a devotional puzzle involving lotuses and deities. This specific problem illustrates the "Inverse Method" (viparīta-vidhi) applied to quadratic equations involving square roots of remainders.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

->( 27 )<-

Worship Ganesha with a single lotus; Shiva <gloss>the Three-eyed One</gloss>, Vishnu <gloss>Hari</gloss>, Brahma, and the Sun <gloss>Lord of the Day</gloss> with three times the square root of the remainder; and Lakshmi <gloss>Kamala</gloss> with one-fifth of the remaining portions; and with one [lotus] worship the pair of lotus-feet of the Teacher <gloss>Guru</gloss>. Tell me quickly the total collection of lotuses.

**Setup:** | 1 | $\frac{\dot{1}}{5}$ | $\frac{\dot{1}}{4}$ | $\frac{\dot{1}}{3}$ | $\frac{\dot{1}}{2}$ | Initial 1 | Final 1 |
<note>The dots over the fractions in the transcription indicate that these are square roots or specific fractional operations within the "Inverse Method." In this context, the setup tracks the stages of the lotuses being distributed.</note>

The quantity of lotuses produced is 36.

### Rule

->“In the case of a root-multiplier, at the top of the root,<-
->multiply by the multiplier according to the previous rule.<-
->The resulting quantity<-
->should be divided by that same multiplier.”<-

---

* <margin>Another reading exists: “And by the previous rule.”</margin>

1 **Here, the initial remainder** = 1 = <term>Initial</term>. **The final remainder** = 1 = <term>Final</term>. **The result arising from the unit** = $\frac{1}{5}$.
<note>The "unit" refers to the starting point of the reverse calculation.</note>

According to the rule, the value of the remainder is: (Result from the unit $\times$ Initial) + Final = $\frac{1}{5}$ + 1 = $\frac{6}{5}$.

(Then, using the rule "By the initial remainder of the square root of the unit," etc.)

The difference = $\frac{1}{Unit}$ = 5; The product = $\frac{6}{5}$ $\div$ Unit = 6.

Then the sum = $\sqrt{Final^2 + 4 \times Product}$ = $\sqrt{25 + 24}$ = $\sqrt{49}$ = 7.
Two quantities are produced: 6 and 1. By the specific rule stating "the root is found in the reduction," the quantity = $6^2$ = 36.
<note>This is a Sanskrit algebraic method for solving a quadratic equation of the form $x - a\sqrt{x} = b$. The author is working backward from the final known number of lotuses to find the original total.</note>

2 **Here is the proof:** The multiplier by which a quantity becomes a square root is called the <term>Multiplier</term> <gloss>gunaka</gloss>. That which is multiplied by the root is known here as the <term>Root-Multiplier</term> <gloss>mūla-gunaka</gloss>.

Therefore, the Quantity = $\frac{y^2}{Multiplier}$. According to the statement...

# Example of Problems Involving Multipliers and Roots

> In a garden, seeing a swan as bright as gold, a group of women [was seen]. One-eighth of the group, and three times the square root of one-sixth of the total group, along with two and three-quarters <gloss>three minus one-quarter</gloss>, went here and there to catch it. Then Damayanti <gloss>the daughter of King Bhima</gloss>, moving with a slow and charming gait to the tinkling sound of her anklets, went and caught the swan. Tell me, how many women were in the group?
> <note>original: "bhīmajā" refers to Damayanti, a famous princess from the Mahabharata known for her beauty and grace.</note>

**Setup:** | Remainder: $\frac{7}{8}$ | Multiplier: $\frac{1}{6}$ | Root-Multiplier: $\frac{11}{4}$ | Visible: 1 |
<note>The "Visible" (dṛśya) is the single woman, Damayanti, who remained to catch the swan after the others dispersed according to the fractions described.</note>

The number of women produced is 66.

And another:

> From a swarm of bees, the square root of the cube of three <gloss>the square root of 27</gloss> went to a cluster of lotuses, and three more bees went there as well. One-half of the remainder went to a *Kutaja* <gloss>a mountain flowering tree</gloss>. Tell me quickly, how many bees were there in the swarm?
<note>This problem requires finding an unknown total where parts of the total are defined by both a square root and a fixed constant.</note>

---

-> $\frac{y^2}{multi} - \frac{a \cdot y^2}{k \cdot multi} + \frac{g}{gh} y = visible$ <-

-> $\therefore y^2 - \frac{a}{k} y^2 + \frac{g}{gh} multi \cdot y = visible \cdot multi$ <-

-> or $y^2 ( 1 - \frac{a}{k} ) + \frac{g}{gh} multi \cdot y = visible \cdot multi$ <-

Therefore, in place of the "remainder of the square" and the "visible remainder," substituting $visible \cdot multi$ [for the visible quantity] and $\frac{g}{gh} multi$ for the multiplier of the root <term>padaguṇaka</term>, and applying the previous method, the quantity becomes $y^2$. Then the desired quantity will be known as $\frac{y^2}{multi}$, as is well known.

* Bhīmajā = Damayanti.

**Setup:** | Multiplier: 3 $\frac{1}{2}$ | Visible: 6 |

The resulting number of bees is 48.

### Rule in the Sārdhagīti Meter

**The denominator divided by its own fractional part is called the <term>Unit-Divisor</term> <note>original: "rūpahara"</note>. This Unit-Divisor, multiplied by two and then by the 'subtracted quantity,' and then added to the square of the Unit-Divisor, is the <term>Sum</term> <note>original: "yoga"</note>. ॥ 42 ॥**

**The 'visible' quantity is added to the square of the 'subtracted quantity,' and then multiplied by the square of the Unit-Divisor; this is the <term>Product</term> <note>original: "ghāta"</note>. The two values for the quantity are then found as before <note>referring to the standard quadratic formula described in verse 35</note>. ॥ 43 ॥**

**An Example in the Class of Subtracted Squares**

> In the assembly of the King of Matsya, during a gathering of wrestlers, the mighty Bhima—having taken the square of [the total number of wrestlers] diminished by six—slew a pair with his two arms and choked one more by the throat. Tell me, how many wrestlers were there in total?
> <note>This problem describes a situation where the total number of wrestlers minus a square of a specific portion results in the three wrestlers Bhima defeated.</note>

---

<margin>1</margin> Here is the <term>Derivation</term> <note>original: "upapatti"</note>: Let the unknown quantity be $x$ <note>original: "yā", short for "yāvat-tāvat", the standard Sanskrit algebraic symbol for a variable</note>. Then, according to the question:
$x - ( \frac{part \cdot x}{den} - subtracted )^2 = x - ( \frac{x}{\frac{den}{part}} - subtracted )^2 = x - ( \frac{x}{Unit-Divisor} - subtracted )^2$
$= x - ( \frac{x - subtracted \cdot Unit-Divisor}{Unit-Divisor} )^2$
$= \frac{1}{Unit-Divisor^2} ( Unit-Divisor^2 \cdot x - (x^2 - 2 \cdot subtracted \cdot Unit-Divisor \cdot x + subtracted^2 \cdot Unit-Divisor^2) ) = visible$

From this, we get: $x^2 - x \{ ( Unit-Divisor^2 + 2 \cdot subtracted \cdot Unit-Divisor ) \} = - Unit-Divisor^2 ( subtracted^2 + visible )$
Or: $x^2 - (Sum)x = -(Product)$
$\therefore x = \frac{ Sum \pm \sqrt{Sum^2 - 4 \cdot Product} }{ 2 }$

Thus, the operations of Verse 35 are produced here; everything is proven.

<summary>This page defines the algebraic steps for solving quadratic equations where a square of a modified unknown is subtracted from the total. It uses an example from the Mahabharata involving the hero Bhima's wrestling exploits to illustrate the "subtracted square" method.</summary>
<keywords>quadratic equations, Bhima, King Matsya, Unit-Divisor, rūpahara, derivation, upapatti, wrestlers, algebra</keywords>

**Setup:** | 1 | $\frac{1}{4}$ | Subtracted: 6 | Square | Visible: 3 |

The number of wrestlers is 52 or 12.
In this case, the second value <note>12</note> is not accepted because it is inconsistent <note>original: "anupapannatvāt"; if there were only 12 wrestlers, the "square of the portion diminished by six" would involve a negative number before squaring, which the author here deems illogical for the physical context of the problem.</note>.
However, in some cases, both are indeed acceptable—

### Example

> From a flock [of swans], the square of one-twentieth of the total minus one, oh friend, went to Lake Manasa. The visible swans remaining are ninety-five. How many were there in the flock?

**Setup:** | 1 | $\frac{1}{20}$ | Subtracted: 1 | Square | Visible: 95 |

The number of swans is 320 or 120.
In this case, both values are acceptable.

### A Problem Involving the Square of a Part

> O young swan, eager for the water in the basin of the Shala tree! Amidst the clamor, the square of one-tenth of a swarm of bees went buzzing to a Jambu tree laden with fragrance. The bees remaining in the lotus pond are counted as the Jinas <note>the Jinas refers to the 24 spiritual teachers of Jainism, used here as a poetic way to state the number 24</note>. How many bees were there in the swarm?

**Setup:** | 1 | $\frac{1}{10}$ | Subtracted: 0 | Visible: 24 | The number of bees is 60 or 40.

“ Here is the **Procedure** <note>original: "karaṇam"</note>. For instance:

In the first example <note>the wrestlers problem</note>, the **Setup** is: 1 | $\frac{1}{4}$. The denominator divided by its own fractional part is $\frac{4}{1}$.

This is called the <term>Unit-Divisor</term> <note>original: "rūpahara"</note>. The 'subtracted' quantity is 6. Multiplied by two, it is 12. Multiplying the Unit-Divisor by this results in 48. The square of the Unit-Divisor is 16. Adding this results in 64, which is the <term>Sum</term> <note>original: "yoga"</note>. When multiplied, the <term>Product</term> <note>original: "ghāta"</note> is 624. Thus, the Sum and Product are 64 and 624. By the <term>Method of Concurrence</term> <note>original: "saṅkramaṇa-vidhi"; a standard algebraic technique to find two unknown quantities when their sum and the difference of their squares (or similar relations) are known</note>, the two quantities are 52 and 12. ”

# Rule

->The visible quantity is divided by the parts;<-
->The sum of their product and unity is then found.<-

**An Illustration of the Multiplication of Parts**

->O Agastya <note>Agastya is a revered Vedic sage; here the mathematician addresses him poetically</note>! The square of one-sixth of a troop of monkeys,<-
->plus one-twelfth of the troop, is wandering on the mountain peaks.<-
->Sixteen of them, my friend, are seen busy playing in the waterfall<-
->on the slope. How many monkeys are there in total?<-

**Setup:** $\frac{1}{6}$ part; $\frac{1}{12}$ part; Visible: 16. The monkeys result in 48 or 24.

In this instance, both values are to be accepted.

### Rule in half a Giti meter

->The visible quantity is divided by the product of the parts,<-
->[resulting in] the quantity added to unity. <note>original: "rūpe... bhājito rāśiḥ"</note> ॥ 44 ॥<-

---

1 **Here is the proof <term>upapatti</term>:**
The quantity is assumed to be the unknown <term>yāvattāvat</term> <note>original: "ya"; the standard Sanskrit algebraic variable, similar to 'x'</note>. Then, according to the statement of the problem:

$ya - \frac{\text{First Num.} \cdot \text{Second Num.}}{\text{First Denom.} \cdot \text{Second Denom.}} \cdot ya^2 = \text{Visible}$

Then: $ya^2 - \frac{\text{First Denom.} \cdot \text{Second Denom.}}{\text{First Num.} \cdot \text{Second Num.}} \cdot ya = -\frac{\text{First Denom.} \cdot \text{Second Denom.}}{\text{First Num.} \cdot \text{Second Num.}} \cdot \text{Visible}$

Or: $ya^2 - \frac{1}{\frac{\text{First Num.}}{\text{First Denom.}} \cdot \frac{\text{Second Num.}}{\text{Second Denom.}}} \cdot ya = -\frac{\text{Visible}}{\frac{\text{First Num.}}{\text{First Denom.}} \cdot \frac{\text{Second Num.}}{\text{Second Denom.}}}$

$ya^2 - (\text{Sum} \cdot ya) = -\text{Product}$

Then: $ya = \frac{\text{Sum} \pm \sqrt{ya^2 - 4 \cdot \text{Product}}}{2}$

Here, the teacher has assumed $\frac{\text{First Num.}}{\text{First Denom.}}$ to be the first part and $\frac{\text{Second Num.}}{\text{Second Denom.}}$ to be the second part <gloss>and thus it is proven.</gloss>

5 **Here is the proof:** Assume the quantity is *ya*. Then by the statement of the problem:

## An Example of the Class of Visible Fractions

*   In a place thick with Shala, Tala, Dalani, and Neepa trees, and filled with the sound of Koel birds—a place that strikes fear into young elephants—there was a swarm of bees <term>ali</term> <note>original: "alinām"</note>. One-sixth of the swarm, multiplied by one-eighth of the swarm, went toward a lotus pond; a certain part <note>the text mentions "guṇalavo," a multiple of a part</note> went to a Trumpet-flower tree <term>pāṭalī</term>. How many bees were there in total?

**Setup:** 1/6 part; 1/8 part; Visible: 3/2. The bees result in 36.

### The Proof
---

> (Unknown — (Unknown · Unknown) / (First part · Second part)) = Unknown / Visible quantity.
> <note>The unknown is referred to as "ya," an abbreviation for yāvattāvat, the standard variable in Sanskrit algebra.</note>
> 
> 1 — 1 / Visible quantity = (Visible quantity — 1) / Visible quantity = Unknown / (First part · Second part)
> 
> Therefore: Unknown = (First part · Second part · (Visible quantity — 1)) / Visible quantity
> 
> = (First part · Second part (1 — 1 / Visible quantity)) / 1
> 
> = (1 — 1 / Visible quantity) / (1 / First part · 1 / Second part)

Here, it is proven that the Teacher <note>referring to the author, Bhaskara II</note> has designated "1 / Visible quantity" as the **visible fraction** and "1 / First part" and "1 / Second part" as the **fractional parts**.

**An alternate reading of the verse:**
->A swarm of bees crowded together, hidden by the Shala and Dalana trees and Koels.<-
<note>original: "śālaśāladalanolakokilāsaṃkulasya kalamīlito'linām"</note>

**Vocabulary used in this section:**
<vocab>class of visible fractions, illustration, setup, proof, bee, Trumpet-flower tree, the unknown quantity, designation of the visible fraction, designation of the fractional part, alternate reading</vocab>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<sig>5</sig>

### Now, an interesting curiosity regarding squares is described.

**Rule in Arya Meter**

> Choose any desired quantity as the first <term>rāśi</term> <note>original: "rāśi," meaning a quantity or number</note>;
> half of its square becomes the second quantity.
> The sum and the difference of the squares of these two,
> when increased by one, will both yield a square root. || 45 ||

**Example**

> O you who know the wonders of squares,
> tell me quickly the many pairs of numbers
> whose sum and difference of squares,
> when increased by one, become perfect squares.
> <note>variant reading: "Tell them many times, O wise one, if you know the curiosity of squares." original: "tau vadānekadhā vidvan vetsi cet kṛtikautukam"</note>

In this case, if the chosen number is 2, the two quantities produced are 2 and 2.
<note>If x = 2, the rule says y = (2²)/2 = 2. Then (2² + 2² + 1) = 9 (square of 3) and (2² - 2² + 1) = 1 (square of 1).</note>

If the chosen number is 3, the quantities are 3 and <gloss>8/2</gloss>.
<note>This appears to be a scribal error for 9/2. If x = 3, y = 3²/2 = 4.5 or 9/2.</note>

If the chosen number is 4, the quantities are 4 and 8.
<note>If x = 4, y = 4²/2 = 8. Then (16 + 64 + 1) = 81 (square of 9) and (64 - 16 + 1) = 49 (square of 7).</note>

If the chosen number is 2 and 1/3, the quantities are <gloss>7/3</gloss> and <gloss>46/18</gloss>.
<note>The fraction 7/3 squared is 49/9. Half of that is 49/18. The text's "46/18" is likely a scribal error for 49/18.</note>

If the chosen number is 2 and 1/2, the quantities are <gloss>5/2</gloss> and <gloss>25/8</gloss>. Thus, by the force of one’s choice, the solutions are infinite.

+ In this rule, one should not choose a number less than two.
<note>If the chosen number is less than 2, the second quantity becomes smaller than the first in a way that might lead to negative results in the subtraction, though the rule still technically holds in absolute terms.</note>

---

<note>1. Here is the proof <term>upapatti</term>: Let the two quantities be 'ya' <note>the unknown</note> and 'ka' <note>a second unknown</note>. According to the problem's statement: ya² ± ka² + 1 must be a square. Therefore, by the method of finding roots, if we set ya² + ka² + 1 = (ya ± 1)², it expands to ya² ± 2ya + 1. Thus, the value of the unknown is ya = <gloss>ka²/2</gloss>. Thus it is proven that the first quantity is the 'chosen' one, and the other is equal to half its square.</note>

<vocab>कृति (kṛti): square; सूत्रमार्या (sūtramāryā): a rule written in the Arya meter; राशि (rāśi): quantity or number; वर्गचमत्कृति (vargacamatkṛti): the wonder or magic of squares; उपपत्ति (upapatti): proof or demonstration; पाठान्तरम् (pāṭhāntaram): variant reading</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a recreational mathematical rule for generating pairs of numbers with specific properties related to their squares, accompanied by worked examples using integers and fractions.</summary>
<keywords>Sanskrit mathematics, Bhaskara II, algebra, perfect squares, Arya meter, mathematical proof, rational numbers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical explorations from page 33. While the previous page discussed generating pairs where the sum and difference of their squares *plus one* resulted in square numbers, this page introduces a new rule (Verse 46) where the sum and difference of squares *minus one* result in squares. It also introduces Verse 47, which explores a trio of conditions: the sum, the difference, and the product of two numbers (plus one) all resulting in perfect squares.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

### Rule in Arya Meter

> Let the first quantity be the cube of any desired number;
> the other should be half of the square of that number's square, increased by one.
> The sum and difference of the squares of these two,
> when decreased by one, will both yield a square root. || 46 ||

### Example

> Tell me quickly the many pairs of numbers
> whose sum and difference of squares, when diminished by one,
> result in perfect squares—if you truly know this curiosity of squares.

In this case, by choosing 1 as the desired number, the two quantities produced are 1 and 3/2.
<note>If a = 1, then x = 1³ = 1, and y = (1⁴ / 2) + 1 = 1.5 or 3/2. Check: (1.5² + 1² - 1) = 2.25 (square of 1.5) and (1.5² - 1² - 1) = 0.25 (square of 0.5).</note>

By choosing 2, the quantities are 8 and 9.
<note>If a = 2, then x = 2³ = 8, and y = (2⁴ / 2) + 1 = 9. Check: (81 + 64 - 1) = 144 (square of 12) and (81 - 64 - 1) = 16 (square of 4).</note>

By choosing 3, the quantities are 27 and 83/2.
<note>If a = 3, then x = 3³ = 27, and y = (3⁴ / 2) + 1 = 40.5 or 83/2.</note>

By choosing 1/2, they are 1/8 and 33/32.
<note>If a = 1/2, then x = (1/2)³ = 1/8, and y = ((1/2)⁴ / 2) + 1 = 1/32 + 1 = 33/32.</note>

By choosing 1/3, they are 1/27 and 82/81.
<note>The rule results in 163/162, but the text provides 82/81, which is likely a variant or scribal simplification.</note>

Thus, by the power of choice, there are many such pairs.

### Rule

> The square of the square of a desired number is written in two places;
> it is decreased by the desired number's square in one, and increased by it in the other, then both are multiplied by two.
> The sum and the difference of these two quantities are squares,
> and their product, when increased by one, also becomes a perfect square. || 47 ||

---

<margin>1</margin> Here is the proof <note>original: "upapatti"</note>. In Bhāskara's rule <note>Referring to the 12th-century mathematician Bhāskara II</note> starting with "The square of the square and the cube of the desired...", if the chosen value is $i/2$, then the quantities are $8(i/2)^4 + 1$ and $8(i/2)^3$, or simply $i^4/2 + 1$ and $i^3$. Thus, the rule is established.

<margin>2</margin> Here is the proof [for Verse 47]. Let the two quantities be represented as:
2 (<term>yāvat-tāvat</term>² + <term>kālaka</term>²) and 2 (<term>yāvat-tāvat</term>² - <term>kālaka</term>²)
<note>The translator uses "yāvat-tāvat" (the first unknown, like 'x') and "kālaka" (the second unknown, like 'y') as per ancient Indian algebraic notation.</note>
In this setup, the first two conditions (sum and difference being squares) are automatically satisfied. Their product, when increased by one, is:
$4 \text{ yā}^4 - 4 \text{ kā}^4 + 1$.
This must be a perfect square. Following the method for extracting square roots:
$2 \times 2 \text{ yā}^2 \times 1 = 4 \text{ yā}^2 = 4 \text{ kā}^4$
Therefore, $y\bar{a} = k\bar{a}^2$.
By substituting this value back, the two quantities become:
2 ($k\bar{a}^4 + k\bar{a}^2$) and 2 ($k\bar{a}^4 - k\bar{a}^2$).
Thus, the rule is proven.

### Example

> If you have practiced sufficiently in the curiosities of squares, 
> tell me: which two numbers are such that their sum, 
> their difference, and their product increased by one, 
> all result in square numbers?

With the chosen number <term>2</term><note>Sanskrit: *dvika*</note>, the two quantities produced are 40 and 24. 
<note>Check: 40 + 24 = 64 (8²); 40 - 24 = 16 (4²); (40 × 24) + 1 = 961 (31²).</note>

With the chosen number 3, the quantities are 180 and 144. 
<note>Check: 180 + 144 = 324 (18²); 180 - 144 = 36 (6²); (180 × 144) + 1 = 25921 (161²).</note>

With [the chosen number] one and a half, the quantities are 117/8 and 45/8. 

Thus, by the power of choice, there are many such pairs.

### Rule in Arya Meter

> Let the sum of the squares of two chosen numbers be the first; 
> twice the product of those same chosen numbers is the other. 
> Separately, their sum and their difference 
> will each result in a square. || 48 ||

### Example

> Tell me, friend, which two numbers are such that 
> their sum and their difference both result in squares? 
> Speak, if you have put true effort into the study of squares.

With the chosen numbers 1 and 2, the two quantities produced are 5 and 4. 
<note>Using the rule: 1² + 2² = 5 (first); 2 × (1 × 2) = 4 (second). Check: 5 + 4 = 9 (3²); 5 - 4 = 1 (1²).</note>

Or, with the chosen numbers 2 and 3, the quantities are 13 and 12. 
<note>Check: 13 + 12 = 25; 13 - 12 = 1.</note>

Or, with the chosen numbers 1 and 3, the quantities are 10 and 6. 
<note>Check: 10 + 6 = 16; 10 - 6 = 4.</note>

Thus, by the power of choice, there are many such pairs.

### Rule in Arya Meter

> The two [numbers] previously mentioned, 
> when multiplied by the square of the cube of a chosen number 
> and divided by the square of their product... 
<note>The text of this rule appears to be a continuation or a modification for a more complex case involving cubes.</note>

---

<note>1. The proof here is quite clear: since (x + y)² = x² + y² + 2xy and (x - y)² = x² + y² - 2xy. Therefore, if the first quantity is (x² + y²) and the second is (2xy), the sum and difference must be squares. Hence it is established.</note>

<note>2. The proof here: If, by the previous rule, the quantities are (x² + y²) and (2xy), and they are multiplied by the square of some desired number n...</note>

<note>original: "उदाहरणम्" (Example), "सूत्रमार्या" (Rule in Arya meter), "उपपत्तिः" (Proof/Derivation), "राशिः" (Quantity/Number), "वर्गः" (Square), "यावत्/कालक" (Variables x and y), "इष्ट" (Desired/Chosen number).</note>

## On the Sum and Difference of Two Quantities
## [Where] the Square and Product result in a Cube || 46 ||

Example:

-> Where the sum and the difference of two quantities are squares, <-
-> and their product is a cube; O friend, if you know this, <-
-> tell those two to me quickly. <-

In this case, as before, let the base quantities be 4 and 5. 
<note>These base values 4 and 5 are derived from the previous rule where chosen numbers 1 and 2 were used (2×1×2=4 and 1²+2²=5). Their sum is 9 and difference is 1, both squares.</note>

From these, by multiplying with a factor derived from the cube of the <term>desired number</term><note>Sanskrit: *iṣṭa*; a number chosen at will by the mathematician to generate a specific solution.</note> ten, the two quantities produced are:

10000 and 12500.
<note>Verification: Sum = 22,500 (150²); Difference = 2,500 (50²); Product = 125,000,000 (500³).</note>

Or, with the cube of five, the quantities are 625/4 and 3125/4. 

Or, let the base quantities be 12 and 13. 
<note>Derived from chosen numbers 2 and 3: 2×2×3=12 and 2²+3²=13. Sum 25, difference 1.</note>

From these, with the cube of the desired ten, the quantities are 250000/507 and 62500/117. 

With the cube of five, they are 15625/2028 and...

---

If the two quantities are assumed to be 2 · <term>yā</term> · <term>kā</term> · <term>nī</term>² and (<term>yā</term>² + <term>kā</term>²) · <term>nī</term>², 
<note>The abbreviations yā (yāvaka/yellow), kā (kālaka/black), and nī (nīlaka/blue) are the standard variables used in Sanskrit algebra, equivalent to x, y, and z.</note>

then the two conditions (sum and difference being squares) are met. Now, their product is:
= <term>nī</term>³ × 2 · <term>yā</term> · <term>kā</term> (<term>yā</term>² + <term>kā</term>²). 

Since this must be a cube, if we set:
<term>nī</term>² = $\frac{( \text{i}^३ )^२}{( २ \text{ या. का. } )^३ \cdot ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ )^२}$
<note>Here 'i' represents the *iṣṭa* or desired number.</note>

Then <term>nī</term>³ = $\frac{( \text{i}^३ )^३}{( २ \text{ या. का. } )^४ ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ )^३}$

Therefore, the product = $\frac{( \text{i}^३ )^४}{( २ \text{ या. का. } )^३ ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ )^२}$ 

This is indeed a cube, because it is equal to:
{ $\frac{\text{i}^{१२}}{\{ २ \text{ या. का } ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ ) \}^३}$ }
= { $\frac{\text{i}^४}{२ \text{ या. का } ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ )}$ } $^३$

Now, since <term>nī</term>² = $\frac{( \text{i}^३ )^२}{( २ \text{ या. का. } )^२ ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ )^२}$
= [ $\frac{( \text{i}^३ )}{२ \text{ या. का } ( \text{या}^२ + \text{का}^२ )}$ ] $^२$

Therefore, if the previously mentioned quantities are multiplied by this, all three conditions are satisfied. Thus, all is proven.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical explorations of the previous section. This type of "Diophantine" problem is a hallmark of the Bijaganita (Algebra) tradition.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>

15625/1282. Thus, there are many possibilities according to one's choice.

### Rule in the Āryā Meter

> The square of the cube of a <term>desired number</term><note>Sanskrit: *iṣṭa*; an arbitrary value chosen by the mathematician to solve the equation.</note> is the first quantity;
> twice that cube is the other; both quantities divided by the <term>square of five</term><note>Sanskrit: *pañcakṛti*; 25.</note>.
> The sum of their squares shall be a cube,
> and the sum of their cubes shall be a square. || 50 ||

---

1. Here is the **mathematical derivation**:
Assume the two quantities are $\frac{i^6}{kā^2}$ and $\frac{yā \cdot i^6}{kā^2}$. 
<note>Here, *i* stands for the desired number (*iṣṭa*), while *yā* (*yāvaka*) and *kā* (*kālaka*) are algebraic variables used by ancient Indian mathematicians to represent unknowns.</note>

The sum of their squares = $i^{12} \left( \frac{1 + yā^2}{kā^4} \right)$. 
In this expression, the first part, $i^{12}$, is certainly a cube <note>because $i^{12} = (i^4)^3$</note>. Therefore, if $\frac{1 + yā^2}{kā^4}$ is also a cube, the condition is satisfied.

Let $\frac{1 + yā^2}{kā^4} = \frac{1}{kā^3}$. Therefore, $1 + yā^2 = kā$.
Consequently, $yā^2 = kā - 1$, and thus $yā = \sqrt{kā - 1}$.

Then the two quantities are $\frac{i^6}{kā^2}$ and $\frac{i^6 \sqrt{kā - 1}}{kā^2}$.
The sum of their cubes = $\frac{i^{18}}{kā^6} \left\{ 1 + (kā - 1)^{\frac{3}{2}} \right\}$.
If this expression $1 + (kā - 1)^{\frac{3}{2}}$ becomes a square, then the second condition is also satisfied.

Now, if we let $kā = 5$:
Then $yā = \sqrt{5 - 1} = 2$.
And $1 + (5 - 1)^{\frac{3}{2}} = 1 + 8 = 9$.
Since 9 is naturally a square, by substituting these values, the two resulting quantities are:
$\frac{i^6}{25}$ and $\frac{2 i^6}{25}$.

Thus, the rule is proven.

<note>original: "इष्टघनवर्ग" (square of the cube of a desired number); "पञ्चकृति" (square of five); "राशी" (two quantities); "वर्गयुति" (sum of squares); "घनयुति" (sum of cubes); "उपपत्ति" (proof/derivation); "सूत्रमार्या" (the rule in Āryā meter).</note>

# An Example

> If you are truly skilled in the **curiosities of squares** <note>Sanskrit: *varga-kautuka*; a term used for recreational or complex square-related mathematics.</note>, friend, tell me quickly:
> Which two numbers are such that their **cubes added together** <note>Sanskrit: *ghanayoge*.</note> result in a square,
> and their **squares added together** <note>Sanskrit: *vargayoge*.</note> result in a cube?

With **one** as the <term>desired number</term><note>Sanskrit: *iṣṭa*; an arbitrary value chosen to initialize the calculation.</note>, the two quantities produced are <gloss>1</gloss>/<gloss>25</gloss> and <gloss>2</gloss>/<gloss>25</gloss>. 
With the number **two**, they are <gloss>64</gloss>/<gloss>25</gloss> and <gloss>128</gloss>/<gloss>25</gloss>.

With **five**, they are 625 and 1250. 
With **one-half**, they are <gloss>1</gloss>/<gloss>1600</gloss> and <gloss>1</gloss>/<gloss>800</gloss>.

With **one-third**, they are <gloss>1</gloss>/<gloss>18225</gloss> and <gloss>2</gloss>/<gloss>18225</gloss>. 
In this manner, by the power of choosing different "desired numbers," there are infinitely many solutions.

# A Rule in the Āryā Meter

> A quantity is multiplied by two different <term>multipliers</term><note>Sanskrit: *guṇaka*.</note>;
> when **twice the multiplier** is added to **unity**, it becomes a square.
> Their sum multiplied by eight, 
> and divided by the square of their difference, is the quantity. || 51 ||

---

1. Here is the **mathematical derivation** <note>Sanskrit: *upapatti*.</note>: 
The two <term>multipliers</term> are designated in order as *a* and *ka* <note>Sanskrit: *a* and *ka* are abbreviations for *guṇaka* (multiplier) and *kālaka* (the second unknown/variable color).</note>. 
The <term>additive constant</term> <note>Sanskrit: *kṣepa*; abbreviated as *kṣe*.</note> is 1.
The unknown <term>quantity</term> <note>Sanskrit: *rāśi*; the number we are trying to find.</note> is *ya* <note>Abbreviation for *yāvattāvat*, the standard Sanskrit algebraic variable like 'x'.</note>.
The <term>square roots</term> are *ra* and *la* <note>Abbreviations for *rāśi* or *mūla*.</note>.

Therefore, we have the following two equations:
(1) $a \cdot ya + 1 = ra^2$
(2) $ka \cdot ya + 1 = la^2$

By taking the difference of these two:
$(a - ka) ya = (ra^2 - la^2) = (ra - la)(ra + la)$

Here, let us assume $(ra - la) = i \cdot (a - ka)$ <note>where *i* is a chosen desired number</note>.
Then $(ra + la) = ya / i$.

By the process of **reconciliation** <note>Sanskrit: *saṅkramaṇa*; a technique to find two values if their sum and difference are known.</note>:
$ra = \frac{1}{2} \{ ya / i + i(a - ka) \} = \frac{ya + i^2(a - ka)}{2i}$

By **squaring** this:
$\frac{ya^2 + 2i^2 \cdot ya(a - ka) + i^4(a - ka)^2}{4i^2} = a \cdot ya + 1$
Refer to equation (1).

->( 36 )<-

# Example

> O learned one, tell me quickly: what is that quantity which, when multiplied by five and three separately and then increased by **one**, becomes a **square** <note>Sanskrit: *mūlada*; literally "that which gives a root," meaning a perfect square.</note>?

The multipliers are 5 and 3. The resulting quantity is 16. <note>Calculation: 16 × 5 + 1 = 81 (9²), and 16 × 3 + 1 = 49 (7²).</note>

---

By <term>clearing the denominator</term><note>Sanskrit: *chedagamena*; the process of removing fractions by multiplying through by a common denominator.</note>:
y² + 2i² y ( a — k ) + i⁴ ( a — k )² = 4ai² y + 4i² kshe.

By <term>transposition</term><note>Sanskrit: *samashodhanena*; literally "equal-clearing," the process of moving terms from one side of an equation to the other.</note>:
y² — 2i² y ( a + k ) = 4i² kshe — i⁴ ( a — k )² .

By <term>completing the square</term><note>Sanskrit: *vargapurane*; an algebraic technique to turn a quadratic expression into a perfect square.</note>:
y² — 2i² y ( a + k ) + i⁴ ( a + k )² = 4i² ( aki² + kshe ) .

By <term>taking the square root</term><note>Sanskrit: *mulagrahana*.</note>:
y — i² ( a + k ) = ± 2i √aki² + kshe

* Therefore: y = i² ( a + k ) ± 2i √aki² + kshe

In the present case, the <term>additive</term><note>Sanskrit: *kṣepa*; the constant value added to the product to make it a square.</note> is 1, so the smallest possible value for the quantity might be zero. This occurs only when:
i² ( a + k ) = 2i √aki² + 1.

And so, by <term>squaring</term><note>Sanskrit: *vargakarana*.</note>:
i⁴ ( a + k )² = 4 aki² + 4 i² .

By <term>transposition</term>: i⁴ ( a — k )² = 4 i²
Thus i² = 4 / ( a — k )². At that point, the smallest non-zero value for the quantity is obtained by <term>substitution</term><note>Sanskrit: *utthāpana*; plugging a found value back into the original formula.</note> as:
Quantity = 8 ( a + k ) / ( a — k )²
This is <term>proven</term><note>Sanskrit: *upapanna*.</note>.

---

> * When the larger and smaller roots are determined for a specified additive in the **Square-nature** <note>Sanskrit: *kṛti-prakṛti*; the traditional Indian method for solving equations of the form Nx² + 1 = y², often called the Pell's Equation in the West.</note>,
> let the product of the two multipliers be assumed as the 'nature.'
> Then, the square of the smaller root, 
> multiplied by the sum of the multipliers and increased or decreased by twice the larger root, 
> becomes the quantity sought here. || This is another method.

It concludes with an alternative method using the "Square-nature" (Kṛti-prakṛti) technique.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of quadratic equations and square roots. <page-num>40</page-num>

### Example

->What quantity, when multiplied by the **Vedas** <note>original: *nigamaiḥ*; the number 4, referring to the four Vedas.</note> and the **mountains** <note>original: *śailaiḥ*; the number 7, referring to the seven mythical mountain ranges.</note> separately and then diminished by **three**,<-
->becomes a **perfect square** <note>Sanskrit: *mūlada*; literally "root-giving."</note>? Tell me, O expert in the wonders of squares.<-

Following the previously stated rule, the quantity is **1**, **21**, or **1057**. <note>Calculation check: For 1: (1×4)-3=1² and (1×7)-3=2². For 21: (21×4)-3=9² and (21×7)-3=12².</note>

---

### Rule in Āryā Meter

->The sum of the two values by which a quantity is increased and decreased to become a square,<-
->is divided by an **assumed number** <note>Sanskrit: *iṣṭa*; a chosen variable used to find a solution.</note>. Subtract that assumed number from the result and halve it.<-
->That result, multiplied by itself and added to the value that was subtracted from the original quantity, gives the **quantity**. || 52 ||<-

---

### Example

->What quantity, when increased by **seventeen** and diminished by **four**, results in a square?<-
->O best of mathematicians, tell me quickly if you know the nature of squares.<-

Given the values 17 and 4:
With **1** as the assumed number, the quantity is **104**.
With **3** as the assumed number, it is **8**.

With **1/2** as the assumed number:
  **6953**
  **——**
   **16**
<note>This represents the fraction 6953/16.</note>

In this manner, by changing the assumed value, many solutions can be found.

---

1. **Here is the proof:** Let the quantity be = *yā* <term>yāvattāvat</term> <note>The standard Sanskrit algebraic symbol for an unknown variable, similar to 'x'.</note>. 
Then, according to the problem:
*yā* + additive₁ = *kā*² <note>*kā* stands for *kālaka*, the "black" unknown, used here as the square root of the first expression.</note>
*yā* - additive₂ = *nī*² <note>*nī* stands for *nīlaka*, the "blue" unknown, used here as the square root of the second expression.</note>

By taking the difference of these two:
additive₁ + additive₂ = *kā*² - *nī*² = ( *kā* + *nī* ) ( *kā* - *nī* ). 

Here, if we let ( *kā* - *nī* ) = *i* <note>The assumed number (*iṣṭa*).</note>, then:

 additive₁ + additive₂
----------------------- = *kā* + *nī* = *p* <note>Sanskrit: *phala*; the quotient or result.</note>
           *i*

*kā* - *nī* = *i*

              *p* - *i*
$\therefore$ *nī* = ———
                2
<note>In the line above, 'no' is written in the manuscript, likely representing 'nī' (nīlaka) in the algebraic derivation.</note>

Since *yā* - additive₂ = *nī*², then: *yā* = *nī*² + additive₂.

Thus, everything is proven.

It introduces the use of colored variables—black and blue—to represent the roots of these squares in the formal proof.</meta>

<sig>6</sig>

# Rule in Āryā Meter

->The difference between two quantities [to be added] is divided by an **assumed number** <note>Sanskrit: *iṣṭa*; a variable chosen by the mathematician to facilitate the solution.</note>.<-
->To this result, add the assumed number and halve it. The square of this result, diminished by the **smaller** <note>The text says *alpavihīno* (diminished by the smaller), though mathematically, to reach the common quantity in the example provided, one subtracts the larger additive from its corresponding square. For example, 16 - 10 = 6.</note> additive, results in the desired **quantity**. || 53 ||<-

# Example

->O friend, tell me the quantity which, when separately increased by **three** and **ten**,<-
->becomes a **square** <note>Sanskrit: *mūlada*; literally "root-giving."</note>? Speak, if you are expert in the science of squares.<-

From the values **10** and **3**, the **quantity** produced is **6**. <note>Calculation: 6 + 10 = 16 (4²) and 6 + 3 = 9 (3²). Using the rule with 1 as the assumed number: [(10-3)/1 + 1] / 2 = 4. Then 4² - 10 = 6.</note>

# Rule in Āryā Meter

->A quantity appearing in two conditions, from which two values are subtracted to result in a perfect square; the difference between those two values...<-

---

1. **Here is the proof:** Let the quantity be = **the unknown** <term>yāvattāvat</term> <note>The standard variable in Indian algebra, literally meaning "as many as," abbreviated here as *ya*.</note>. Then, according to the statement of the problem:
*ya* + <term>additive</term>₁ <note>Sanskrit: *kṣepa*; a constant added to an equation.</note> = <term>black</term>² <note>Sanskrit: *kālaka*; the second unknown variable, used here to represent the root of the first square. Abbreviated as *ka*.</note>
*ya* + additive₂ = <term>blue</term>² <note>Sanskrit: *nīlaka*; the third unknown variable, representing the root of the second square. Abbreviated as *ni*.</note>
By the difference: additive₁ - additive₂ = *ka*² - *ni*² = ( *ka* + *ni* ) ( *ka* - *ni* )
If *ka* - *ni* = **an assumed number** <term>iṣṭa</term> <note>Abbreviated as *i*.</note>, then:
  additive₁ - additive₂
 ——————— = **result** <note>Sanskrit: *phala*; the quotient of the division. Abbreviated as *fa*.</note> = *ka* + *ni*
      *i*
      *i* = *ka* - *ni*
Then *result* + *assumed number* = 2 *ka*
       *result* + *assumed number*
∴ *ka* = —————
                2
Similarly, the quantity *ya* = *ka*² - additive₁. Here, if the **assumed number** *i* = 1, then the rule stated by the **Teacher** <note>Sanskrit: *ācārya*; referring to the author of the foundational rules, likely Bhāskara II.</note> is established.

2. **Here is the proof [for subtraction]:** Let the quantity be = *ya*. Then, according to the statement of the problem:
*ya* - additive₁ = *ka*²
*ya* - additive₂ = *ni*²

<summary>This page presents a mathematical rule and algebraic proof for finding a number that becomes a perfect square when two different constants are added to it, illustrated by the example of the number 6 (where 6+10=16 and 6+3=9).</summary>
<keywords>algebra, quadratic equations, square roots, yāvattāvat, kālaka, nīlaka, additive (kṣepa), assumed number (iṣṭa), Bhāskara, Bijaganita</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical rules from page 41. It concludes the procedure for finding a single number that becomes a perfect square when two different values are subtracted from it (Rule 54), provides a practical example, and then transitions into Rule 55, which addresses a more complex algebraic identity involving the sum of squares and products of two unknown quantities.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

...[the difference between the two subtrahends is] decreased by **one** <note>original: *vyeka*</note> and **halved** <note>original: *dalita*</note>; the **square** <note>original: *varga*</note> of this result, when added to the **larger subtracted value** <note>Sanskrit: *analpa-sameto*; literally "combined with the not-small."</note>, becomes the desired **quantity**. || 54 ||

# Example

->O friend, what quantity, when separately diminished by **twenty-seven** and **four**,<-
->becomes a **square**? Tell me, if you possess skill in the **science of arithmetic** <term>pāṭyām</term> <note>Specifically refers to *Pāṭīgaṇita*, the branch of mathematics dealing with algorithms and arithmetic.</note>.<-

**Statement: 4, 27. The resulting quantity is 148.**
<note>Calculation: The difference is 27 - 4 = 23. Following the rule: (23 - 1) / 2 = 11. Then, 11² + 27 = 121 + 27 = 148. Verification: 148 - 27 = 121 (11²) and 148 - 4 = 144 (12²).</note>

# Rule in Āryā Meter

->Whatever the first [quantity] is, its **square** <note>original: *varga*</note> is decreased by its own **fourth part** <note>original: *svāṅghryūna*</note> and divided by an **assumed number** <note>original: *iṣṭa*</note>.<-
->Subtract [the assumed number] from the first, then halve it to find the other. This is called the **sum of squares**; the product is a square. || 55 ||

---

By the difference: **Additive₂ - Additive₁ = Black² - Blue² = (Black + Blue) × (Black - Blue)**
<note>The mathematician uses "color" names for variables: *kālaka* (Black) and *nīlaka* (Blue) represent the roots of the two resulting squares.</note>

Even here, if **Black - Blue = Assumed Number**,
then **(Additive₂ - Additive₁) / Assumed Number = Result = Black + Blue**
$\phantom{then }$ **Assumed Number = Black - Blue**
$\therefore$ **(Result - Assumed Number) / 2 = Blue**
And also: **Unknown = Blue² + Additive₂**.
If the **Assumed Number** is **1**, then the rule stated by the **Teacher** <note>Refers to the author of the core verses, likely Bhāskara II.</note> is proven.

1. **Here is the proof:**
Let the two quantities be the **Unknown** <term>yāvattāvat</term> <note>The primary variable in Indian algebra, abbreviated as *ya*.</note> and the **Black** <term>kālaka</term> <note>The second variable, abbreviated as *ka*.</note>. Then, according to the question:
**Unknown² + (Unknown × Black) + Black² = Blue²**

Or **{ Unknown + (Black / 2) }² + (3 × Black² / 4) = Blue²**
<note>This shows the technique of "completing the square" to solve quadratic equations.</note>

Or **(3 × Black² / 4) = Blue² - { Unknown + (Black / 2) }²**

<summary>This page concludes the rules for solving "double-subtraction" square problems and introduces Rule 55, which uses algebraic proofs involving "colored" variables (Black and Blue) to solve complex quadratic identities.</summary>
<keywords>subtraction rule, square roots, arithmetic of squares, Bhāskara II, algebraic proof, variables, completing the square</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of quadratic problems. It then concludes that section and begins a new topic regarding the equality of products and sums of unknowns.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

### Example

> Which two quantities’ **sum of squares** <note>original: *varga-yogaḥ*</note>, when increased by their **product** <note>original: *ghāta*</note>, becomes a **square** <note>literally "gives a root" (*pada-pradaḥ*)</note>? Tell me that quickly, if you are curious about the **wonders of squares** <term>varga-kutuka</term>.

Let the first [quantity] be imagined as any number, such as **12**. With **one** as the **assumed number** <term>iṣṭa</term> <note>A common technique in Indian mathematics where a seed value is chosen to solve an indeterminate equation.</note>, the two quantities produced are **12** and **5/2**. To avoid fractions <note>original: *abhinnārthaṃ*; literally "for the sake of non-division"</note>, these are doubled to become **24** and **5**. 

Using **two** as the assumed number, the quantities produced are **12** and **20**. Where possible, these are reduced <note>original: *apavartitau*; simplified by a common divisor.</note> by **four**, resulting in **3** and **5**. In this way, an infinite number of solutions can be found depending on the assumed number. The remainder of this is useful for **geometry** <term>kṣetra</term>, and I shall discuss it in that section.

->Thus ends the Wonder of Squares.<-

### Now, the equality of the product and sum, etc., is discussed.
### Rule in Āryā Meter.

**The sum of two assumed numbers,**
**divided by the assumed number and then by two.** <note>The verse fragment here is partially technical; it refers to the algebraic manipulation of variables represented by colors.</note>

---

$= \{ \text{Blue} + ( \text{First Unknown} + \frac{\text{Black}}{2} ) \} \{ \text{Blue} - ( \text{First Unknown} + \frac{\text{Black}}{2} ) \}$ 
<note>The original uses the shorthand for the variables: *yā* for *yāvat-tāvat* (First Unknown), *kā* for *kālaka* (Black), and *nī* for *nīlaka* (Blue).</note>

In this case, if $\text{Blue} - ( \text{First Unknown} + \frac{\text{Black}}{2} ) = \text{Assumed Number}$ <note>original: *i* (*iṣṭa*)</note>

Then $\frac{\frac{3 \text{ Black}^2}{4}}{\text{Assumed}} = \text{Blue} + ( \text{First Unknown} + \frac{\text{Black}}{2} ) = \text{Quotient}$ <note>original: *pha* (*phala*)</note>

By the **rule of concurrence** <term>saṅkramaṇa</term> <note>An algebraic process for finding two numbers when their sum and difference are known: $(s+d)/2$ and $(s-d)/2$.</note>: 
$\frac{\text{Quotient} - \text{Assumed}}{2} = \text{First Unknown} + \frac{\text{Black}}{2}$

$\therefore \text{First Unknown} = \frac{\text{Quotient} - \text{Assumed} - \text{Black}}{2}$

Thus, the proof is established.

**1.** Here is the **proof** <term>upapatti</term>: Let the two quantities be the **First Unknown** <gloss>yā</gloss> and the **Black** <gloss>kā</gloss>. Then, according to the statement of the problem:
$\text{First Unknown} + \text{Black} = \text{First Unknown} \times \text{Black}$ 
$\therefore \text{First Unknown} = (\text{First Unknown} \times \text{Black}) - \text{Black} = \text{Black} \times (\text{First Unknown} - 1)$
$\therefore \text{Black} = \frac{\text{First Unknown}}{\text{First Unknown} - 1}$. 
By this method, another variation is established.

<keywords>sum of squares, product, assumed number (iṣṭa), rule of concurrence (saṅkramaṇa), color variables, algebraic proof (upapatti)</keywords>

<page-num>44</page-num>

( 44 )

...by the practice [of these methods], my [knowledge] 
certainly becomes complete in all ways. || 56 ||

### Example
->Which two quantities are such that their **sum** and **product** are equal?<-
->Tell me quickly, if you have put effort into this mathematical work.<-

With the **assumed values** <term>iṣṭa</term> <note>seed values chosen by the mathematician to generate solutions</note> being 1 and 1, the two **quantities** <note>original: *rāśī*</note> produced are **2** and **2**. 
Or, with assumed values 1 and 2, the quantities are **3** and **3/2**. 
Or, with 3 and 4, the quantities are **7/3** and **7/4**.
In this way, by the power of the assumed values, there is an **infinity of solutions**.

### Rule in Āryā Meter
> "The sum [of the assumed numbers] multiplied by each,
> divided by the sum of their squares, are the two quantities.
> Then, the **sum of their squares**
> becomes equal to their **sum**."³ || 56 ||

---

Now, if <insert>the First Unknown</insert> + <insert>the Second Unknown</insert> = <insert>the First Unknown</insert> × <insert>the Second Unknown</insert> 
<note>original variables: *yā* (yāvat-tāvat) and *kā* (kālaka). In Indian algebra, different colors were used to represent unknown variables; *yāvat-tāvat* means "as many as" (x) and *kālaka* means "black" (y).</note>
Therefore, (First + Second) / First = Second
Similarly, (First + Second) / Second = First
Thus, the First and Second unknowns can be set as any assumed values.
By this logic, the first method is established.

* A first quantity is assumed; that same quantity divided by the assumed value minus one is the other quantity. This is another method.

<margin>1. Here is the **demonstration** <term>upapatti</term>. Let the two quantities be imagined as *yā* and *kā*. Then, according to the question:
*yā²* + *kā²* = *yā* + *kā*
Multiply both sides by *yā*: *yā* (*yā²* + *kā²*) = *yā* (*yā* + *kā*). Therefore, *yā* = [*yā* (*yā* + *kā*)] / [*yā²* + *kā²*]
Similarly, *kā* (*yā²* + *kā²*) = *kā* (*yā* + *kā*). Therefore, *kā* = [*kā* (*yā* + *kā*)] / [*yā²* + *kā²*]
Thus, the rule is demonstrated.</margin>

<margin>2. "Multiplying the sum of the assumed values." 3. "The sum is produced equal." These are alternate readings of the text.</margin>

<summary>This page explores two algebraic problems: finding numbers whose sum equals their product, and Rule 56, which provides a formula for finding numbers where the sum of their squares equals their sum.</summary>
<keywords>Bijaganita, Sanskrit mathematics, Bhaskara II, algebra, yavat-tavat, kalaka, sum and product, sum of squares, indeterminate equations, Upapatti</keywords>

<page-num>45</page-num>

## Example

->Which two quantities are such that the **sum of their squares** is equal to their **sum**, O friend?<-
->Tell me quickly in various ways, if you possess skill in this mathematical art.<-

With the **assumed seed values** <term>iṣṭa</term> 1 and 2, the two **quantities** <term>rāśī</term> produced are:
<span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">3<br>—<br>5</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">6<br>—<br>5</span>

<note>To verify: (3/5)² + (6/5)² = 9/25 + 36/25 = 45/25 = 9/5. Also, 3/5 + 6/5 = 9/5. The sum of the squares equals the sum.</note>

Alternatively, with the assumed values 2 and 3, the two quantities produced are:
<span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">10<br>—<br>13</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">15<br>—<br>13</span>

In this way, by the power of choosing different assumed values, there is an **infinity of solutions**.

## Rule in Āryā Meter

+ "When the **assumed values** are multiplied by the product of the **sum of their squares** and the **sum of the values**, and then divided by the **sum of their cubes**, the results are the two quantities. In these, the **sum of their cubes** becomes equal to the **sum of their squares**." || 58 ||

---

### Derivation
<note>original: *upapattiḥ*. In Indian mathematics, this section provides the logical reasoning or "proof" for why the rule works.</note>

Let the two quantities be represented as <term>yā</term> <note>the First Unknown, shorthand for *yāvat-tāvat*, meaning "as many as"</note> and <term>का</term> <note>the Second Unknown, shorthand for *kālaka*, meaning "black"</note>.
Then, according to the question:
*yā*³ + *kā*³ = *yā*² + *kā*²

Or, multiplying both sides by *yā*:
*yā* ( *yā*² + *kā*² ) = *yā* ( *yā*³ + *kā*³ )

Therefore:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*yā* ( *yā*² + *kā*² )
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;——————————————————— = *yā*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*yā*³ + *kā*³

Similarly for the second quantity:
*kā* ( *yā*² + *kā*² ) = *kā* ( *yā*³ + *kā*³ )

Therefore:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*kā* ( *yā*² + *kā*² )
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;——————————————————— = *kā*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*yā*³ + *kā*³

In the same way, for a **second problem** <note>where the sum of cubes equals the square of the sum</note>:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*yā* ( *yā* + *kā* )²
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;——————————————————— = *yā*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*yā*³ + *kā*³

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*kā* ( *yā* + *kā* )²
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;——————————————————— = *kā*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*yā*³ + *kā*³

Thus, for other such questions as well, the quantities are determined.

• <margin>Variant reading: "When the **assumed values** are multiplied by the product of the **sum of their squares** and the **sum of the values** and divided by the **cube of the sum of the cubes**..."</margin> <note>original: *pāṭhāntaram*</note>

### The Eight Specific Examples
<note>original: *uddeśakāṣṭakam*. A collection of eight mathematical problems or "challenges" presented for the student to solve.</note>

> Tell me, O mathematician, those two quantities where:
> 1. The square of the sum of their squares is equal to the sum of their cubes,
> 2. Their product is equal to the sum of their cubes, and
> 3. The cube of their sum is equal to the sum of their cubes.

In the first example, to make the **sum of the cubes** <term>ghana-aikya</term> equal to the sum of the squares, using the **assumed seeds** <term>iṣṭa</term> 1 and 2, the two **quantities** produced are <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">5<br>—<br>6</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">10<br>—<br>6</span>.

For the seeds 1 and 2, to make the sum of the cubes equal to the **square of the sum**, the quantities are 1 and 2.

For the seeds 1 and 2, to make the sum of the cubes equal to the **product** <term>ghāta</term>, the quantities produced are <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">2<br>—<br>6</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">4<br>—<br>6</span>.

Alternatively, to make the sum of the cubes equal to the **cube of the sum**, with the seeds 1 and 2, the quantities produced are <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">5<br>—<br>27</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">17<br>—<br>27</span>. <note>These results are derived from substituting specific values into algebraic formulas similar to those on the previous page.</note>

To make the sum of the cubes equal to the square of the sum, the quantities are <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">1<br>—<br>3</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">2<br>—<br>3</span>.

To make the sum of the cubes equal to the product, the quantities are <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">2<br>—<br>27</span> and <span style="display:inline-block; vertical-align:middle; text-align:center;">4<br>—<br>27</span>.

In this manner, other quantities such as those involving differences can be understood by applying one's own intelligence.

**This concludes the section on finding quantities equal to sums and other properties.**

### Now, the Rule for the Inverse Method in Āryā Meter
<note>original: *vyasta-vidhi*. This is the "Method of Working Backwards." If a problem tells you what happens to an unknown number to get a final result, you reverse every operation (addition becomes subtraction, etc.) to find the original number.</note>

> * In the inverse method, a square becomes a square root, and a root becomes a square.
> A divisor becomes a multiplier, and a multiplier becomes a divisor.
> Addition becomes subtraction, and subtraction becomes addition.
> In cases of fractional increase or decrease, the denominator is increased or decreased by its own part <note>the numerator</note> and then the quantity is divided. || 59 ||

**Example**

> † What is that quantity which, when multiplied by four, then decreased by three of its own fourth parts,
> ( then increased by six, then multiplied by itself <note>squared</note>, then divided by six, then increased and decreased by its own third part... )

---

* <margin>This rule—"a divisor as a multiplier, a multiplier as a divisor," and so on—is exactly as stated by the great mathematician Bhaskara.</margin>
† <margin>There is a gap in the verse in the manuscript. Therefore, the portion within the parentheses has been inserted by the editor for clarity.</margin>

> What number, multiplied by four, diminished by three-quarters of itself, increased by six, then squared, divided by sixty, and increased by its own third—if the square root of that result, minus two, quickly leaves a remainder of one, tell me that number, O expert, if your practice of mathematics is vast.

**Statement:** Multiplier 4; Subtraction $\frac{3}{4}$ <note>original: *ṛ* (negative/subtraction)</note>; Addition 6 <note>original: *dha* (positive/addition)</note>; Square; Divisor 60; Addition $\frac{2}{3}$ <note>This "2/3" in the working notes appears to correspond to the "third" mentioned in the verse, likely reflecting a specific step in the calculation</note>; Root; Subtraction 2;
Result 1. The resulting number is 12.

## Now, the Verse for the Rule of Three.

<insert>+</insert> ->The **Measure** and the **Desire** at the beginning and end<-
->are of the same kind, while the **Fruit** is of a different kind.<-
->In the middle is the Fruit; multiplied by the Desire<-
->and divided by the Measure, the result of the Desire is found. ॥ 60 ॥<-
<note>This is the classic *Trairāśika* (Rule of Three). You take three known values to find a fourth. The **Measure** (pramāṇa) and **Desire** (icchā) must share units (e.g., both are "number of fruits"), and the **Fruit** (phala) is the known result (e.g., "price").</note>

## Example.

> Eight coconuts are obtained for five <term>paṇas</term>.
> Tell me, my friend, what is the price for forty fruits?

**Statement:** 8 | 5 | 40. The result is <insert>*</insert> 2 <term>drammas</term> and 1 <term>paṇa</term>.

And also:

> Eight and a half <term>palas</term> of saffron are bought for three
> and a quarter <term>paṇas</term>. Tell me, what amount is obtained for ten
> <term>paṇas</term> diminished by one-sixth?

**Statement:** 3 $\frac{1}{4}$ | 8 $\frac{1}{2}$ | 10 $\frac{1}{6}$

The result is 25 <term>palas</term>, 2 <term>karṣas</term>, 12 <term>māṣas</term>, 
4 <term>guñjās</term>, and $\frac{26}{36}$ parts of a <term>guñjā</term>.

---

<insert>+</insert> This verse, "The measure and desire are of the same kind," etc., is stated by **Bhāskara** himself. <note>Referring to Bhāskara II, the famous 12th-century mathematician.</note>
<insert>*</insert> Here, twelve <term>paṇas</term> make one <term>dramma</term>; for that, one should see the definitions of units.

> If nine and a half <note>original: *bilvādhikasya*—increased by a *bilva* (1 *pala*), added to eight and a half *palas*</note> <term>palas</term> of *guggulu* <note>a fragrant medicinal resin</note> are obtained for three <term>paṇas</term>, tell me quickly, friend, what is the price for one hundred <term>palas</term> of that substance?

**The result is:** <term>drammas</term> 3, <term>paṇas</term> 6, <term>kākiṇī</term> 0, <term>varāṭaka</term> 0, and the fractional part of a <term>varāṭaka</term> is 21/320.

And also:

> If three <term>kuḍavas</term> diminished by three-quarters of a measure are obtained for four <term>drammas</term> minus a tenth part, tell me quickly what is the price for one <term>khārikā</term>?

**Statement:** 39 / 10 | 9 / 4 | 96 / 1 <note>These represent 3.9 drammas, 2.25 kuḍavas, and the 96 kuḍavas that make up one khārikā.</note>

The values at the beginning and end are converted to fourths: 39 / 10 | 9 / 4 | 336 / 1

**The result is:** <term>drammas</term> 26, <term>paṇa</term> 1, <term>kākiṇī</term> 1, and <term>varāṭakas</term> 15 and 1/5.

And also:

> If a female snake moves one <term>kara</term> <note>a cubit, roughly 18-24 inches</note> increased by three and a half <term>aṅgulas</term> <note>finger-breadths</note> in half a day, in what time will she travel five and a half <term>yojanas</term>?

**Vocabulary:** <term>dramma</term> <note>silver coin</note>, <term>paṇa</term> <note>copper coin, 1/16th of a dramma</note>, <term>kākiṇī</term> <note>1/4th of a paṇa</note>, <term>varāṭaka</term> <note>a cowrie shell, 1/20th of a kākiṇī</note>, <term>kuḍav</term> <note>a measure of volume</note>, <term>khārikā</term> <note>a large measure of volume, equal to 96 kuḍavas</note>, <term>pala</term> <note>a unit of weight</note>, <term>aṅgula</term> <note>a finger's breadth</note>, <term>kara</term> <note>a cubit</note>, <term>yojana</term> <note>a long distance measure, roughly 8-9 miles</note>, <term>nyāsa</term> <note>mathematical statement or layout</note>, <term>guggulu</term> <note>medicinal resin</note>.

<page-num>46</page-num>

[Tell me] the quantity whose square root, when decreased by the "element-part" <note>original: *tattva-khaṇḍasya*—referring to the number 25 (the 25 *tattvas* or elements in Samkhya philosophy)</note> and then multiplied by two, quickly becomes the [original] form? Tell me that quantity, O learned one, if your practice of mathematics is extensive.

**Statement:** Multiplier 4; share 3/4; Cube 6, Square-root 6; share 2/3; Weight; Root-subtraction 2; Constant 1. The resulting quantity is 12. <note>This "statement" section follows specific shorthand for solving algebraic equations prevalent in medieval Indian mathematics.</note>

**Statement:** 1/3; 1/2; 5 1/2. The finger-breadths <note>original: *aṅgulas*</note> at the beginning and end are 55/2; 1/2. The 5.5 <term>yojanas</term> result in 4,224,000 units. The time elapsed is 213 years and 4 months. <note>This completes the snake problem from page 45, calculating the time taken to travel a massive distance at a very slow pace.</note>

->Thus ends the Rule of Three.<-

---

### Verse on the Rule for the Inverse Rule of Three.

> In matters of price, living beings <note>original: *asūnāṃ*—referring to slaves or livestock</note>, and age;
> in the fineness of gold and its weight;
> in the measurement of grain and similar goods using <term>kuḍavas</term>—
> when there is a difference in the measure, the inverse rule is applied. || 61 ||

### Example.

> If a sixteen-year-old woman, accessible through charm, jokes, and playfulness, receives a wage of ten <term>niṣkas</term>, then tell me quickly, O best of mathematicians, what wage should be paid by a gallant <note>original: *viṭena*—a man about town or client</note> <insert>Alternative reading: by an actor <note>original: *naṭena*</note></insert> for a twenty-year-old woman?

**Statement:** 16 | 10 | 20. The resulting wage is 8 <term>niṣkas</term>. <note>In the Inverse Rule of Three, as age increases, the wage decreases proportionally (16 × 10 ÷ 20 = 8).</note>

**And also:**

> If a two-wheeled cart <note>original: *dvidhūrūkṣā*</note> is obtained for eight <term>niṣkas</term>, my friend, tell me how many <term>niṣkas</term> would be required for a side-load or a single-yoke cart?

---

### Verse on the Rule of Five and others.

> Having exchanged the denominators and the results <note>original: *phala*—the fruit or outcome of the proportion</note> between the two sides,
> multiply the set with fewer terms and divide the product of the set with more terms by it;
> the result for the Rule of Five, Seven, Nine, and so on, is thus obtained. || 62 ||

### Example.

> If the interest on one hundred is five for one month, tell me quickly, O learned one, what is the interest on sixty-five for one full year? Also, tell me the principal if the interest is known, and tell me the time if you know the principal and the interest—if you truly understand the <term>pāṭī</term> <note>Arithmetic, specifically "board-work" or calculations performed on a dust-covered board</note>.

<vocab>
**Nyāsa** (Statement): The formal layout of a mathematical problem on the calculation board.
**Kalāntara** (Interest): The profit earned on a principal sum over time.
**Mūladhana** (Principal): The original sum of money lent or invested.
**Māsa** (Month): A standard unit of time in financial calculations.
**Pāṭī** (Arithmetic): Short for Pāṭīgaṇita, the traditional science of algorithmic calculation.
**Paṇa**: A denomination of copper coinage.
**Dramma**: A silver coin, usually worth multiple paṇas.
</vocab>

# Example of the Rule of Seven

> If a cloth three hands <note>original: *hasta*, a unit of length roughly 18 inches</note> wide and three-squared <note>nine</note> hands long is obtained for ten *purāṇas* <note>a type of silver coin</note>, what then is the price of three cloths, each twelve hands long and five wide?

**Statement** <note>original: *nyāsa*, the formal layout of numerical values for calculation</note>
| 3 | 5 |
| 8 | 12 |
| 10 | 3 |
| 10 | |
<note>The table represents the proportions between width, length, quantity, and price. Note: The OCR value '8' in the second row likely refers to the '9' mentioned in the verse as 'three-squared'.</note>

The result is 1 *niṣka*, 3 *drammas*, and 8 *paṇas*. <note>These are traditional Indian currency denominations.</note>

# Instruction on the Rule of Nine

> If two pieces of timber, each two hands wide, nine hands long, and one hand thick, are obtained for eight *drammas*, then, O clear-minded one, tell me quickly—if you know arithmetic <note>original: *pāṭī*</note>—what is the price of timber three wide, twelve long, and two thick?

**Statement**
| 2 | 3 |
| 9 | 12 |
| 1 | 2 |
| 2 | |
| 8 | |

The result is 2 *niṣkas* and 4 *drammas*.

# Example of the Rule of Eleven

> If a cart receives six *paṇas* to move a pillar fifteen high and one-and-a-half <note>original: *guṇakara-vyāsārdha-piṇḍa*</note> in thickness for a distance of five *krośas* <note>a unit of distance, roughly 2 miles</note>, then friend, tell me quickly—if you know arithmetic—what is the hire for moving ten timbers, each eighteen high and three in thickness, for a distance of eight *krośas*?

**Statement**
| 15 | 18 |
| $3 \over 2$ | 6 |
| $1 \over 2$ | $3 \over 2$ |
| 1 | 10 |
| 5 | 8 |
| 5 | |
<note>This complex table arranges eleven different variables to calculate the final transport cost.</note>

The resulting hire is 3 *niṣkas*, 6 *drammas*, 4 *paṇas*, 3 *kākiṇīs*, and 4 *varāṭikās* <note>cowrie shells used as the smallest unit of currency</note>.

# Rule for Exchange

> When the prices are swapped and divided by their own values, the method for exchanging commodities <note>original: *bhāṇḍa-pratibhāṇḍaka*, literally "goods for counter-goods"</note> proceeds as previously described in the Rule of Five.

# Example

> Three hundred pomegranates are obtained for one *dramma* <note>a silver coin</note>, and in another market, one hundred mangoes less five <note>95</note> are obtained for one *paṇa* <note>a copper coin, a fraction of a *dramma*</note>. Tell me, friend, if ten pomegranates are given in exchange, how many mangoes should be received?

**Statement** <note>original: *nyāsa*, the numerical layout. Here, the calculation accounts for the different market rates and the currency conversion. Based on the result of 38, this specific text assumes a conversion rate of 1 *dramma* = 12 *paṇas*.</note>

| 1 | 1 | |
|---|---|---|
| 300 | 12 | The resulting mangoes are 38. |
| 10 | 95 | |

->Thus ends the Rule for Exchange.<-

# Now, the Rule for the Sale of Living Beings

> When dealing with the purchase of living beings <note>original: *prāṇikraya*</note>, the rule remains as before, but a reversal is applied to the ratio of their ages. <note>This refers to inverse proportion; generally, in these historical contexts, the value of livestock or servants changed inversely with age after a certain point of maturity.</note>

# Example

> If a pair of women, sixteen years of age, costs sixty *niṣkas* <note>a high-value gold coin</note>, tell me, O best of calculators, what is the price of six beautiful-eyed women, each six years of age, whose gait is slow from the weight of their hips? <note>Such poetic descriptions of women were standard in Sanskrit mathematical word problems, which often used "beautiful-eyed" or "graceful" as stock epithets for subjects.</note>

---

<note>
1. This rule is exactly the same as the one stated by Bhāskara <note>referring to Bhāskara II, the 12th-century mathematician</note> regarding "the method for commodities and counter-commodities."
There is a variant reading: "When the price is set by reversal, the method for exchange of goods is the same."
</note>

## Now, the Operations on Mixtures.

### The Formula.

* The individual investments <note>original: *prakṣepa*, literally "contributions"</note>, when divided by their sum and multiplied by the mixture <note>the total amount of capital plus profit</note>, result in the individual shares.

### Example.

> O learned one, for those whose capital amounts are measured by three, five, seven, and nine—each multiplied by nine—the total sum of capital and profit has become four hundred less sixteen <note>384</note>. Tell me quickly the share of each.

**Statement:** 27 | 45 | 63 | 81 <note>These represent the investments: 3x9, 5x9, 7x9, and 9x9</note>. **Total Mixture:** 384. 
The individual sums of capital and profit are 48, 80, 112, and 144. When the original capital amounts are subtracted from these, the individual profits are found to be 21, 35, 49, and 63. 

Alternatively, the sum of the capital amounts is 216. Subtracting this from the total mixture (384), the sum of the profits is 168. From this, using the rule as before, the individual profits are the same: 21, 35, 49, and 63.

---

* "The investments multiplied by the mixture," etc., is exactly in accordance with what was stated by Bhaskara <note>referring to Bhaskara II, author of the *Lilavati*</note>.

### Now, the Rules for Purchase and Sale.

**Formula:**

> The mixture shall be the sequential price;
> The purchase is the mixture, while the sale is the capital.
> The capital is the selling price;
> The purchase minus the sale is the profit <note>original: *lābha*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exploration of trade and proportions. It builds upon the "Rule of Distribution" (prakṣepa-karaṇa) established on the previous page, applying it now to the specific context of commercial transactions—specifically calculating purchase quantities, sales, and profits.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>

Whatever result is unknown should be understood by the rule of proportion, through the process of distribution as described before, starting from a mixture of unity.

## Example

> Having purchased loads of rice at the rate of eight [per unit of currency], they were sold at the rate of five. The profit made here is sixty. Tell me, what were the total amounts?

**Statement:** Purchase 8, Sale 5, Profit 60. Here, by the rule "the mixture is for the purpose of purchase," etc., the purchase quantity is referred to as the "mixed wealth" <note>original: *miśra-dhana*; in this context, it represents the total quantity of goods handled</note>. The purchase rate minus the sale rate is the profit. If for this profit of 3 <note>8 - 5 = 3</note>, the mixed wealth and the principal capital <note>original: *mūla-dhana*; here representing the quantity equivalent to the cost price</note> are 8 and 5, then for a profit of 60, what would they be? Through the rule of three <note>a mathematical proportion: (60 × 8) ÷ 3 and (60 × 5) ÷ 3</note>, the mixed wealth and the principal are found to be 160 and 100.

Now, for a setup where the purchase rate is unknown: Purchase 8 <note>the value to be verified</note>, Sale 5, Mixed wealth and principal 160 and 100. The proportion is as follows: If for a sale of 100, the purchase is 160, then for a sale of 5, what is the purchase? The purchase rate is thus found to be 8.

In a setup where the sale rate is unknown: Purchase 8, Sale 0 <note>the unknown</note>, Mixed wealth and principal 160 and 100. The proportion is as follows: If for a purchase of 160, the sale is 100, then for a purchase of 8, what is the sale? The sale rate is thus found to be 5.

Moreover:

> O friend, the purchase rates for loads of rice, wheat, and <term>kulamāṣa</term> <note>a type of wild bean or pulse</note> are respectively the numbers of the <term>Rāmas</term> (3), the <term>Bāṇas</term> (5), and the mountains (7) <note>These are "bhūta-saṅkhyā" or symbolic word-numbers: there are 3 Rāmas, 5 arrows/Bāṇas of Kama, and 7 primary mountains</note>. The sale rates are measured by the earth (1), the hands (2), and the qualities <note>implied sequence: 3</note>. Tell me, what is the individual wealth when the profit is equal? Tell me how the sums would be if they were equal when increased or decreased by the profit.

| | 3 | 5 | 7 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Statement** | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| | 1 | 1 | 1 |

The mixed wealth of the capitals is 66. Here, from the proportion, the profit on unity...

<page-num>57</page-num>

The roots <note>original: *mūlāni*; referring to the starting values or proportions</note> 1/2, 2/3, and 3/4, through the process of distribution <note>original: *prakṣepa-karaṇa*; a method for proportional allocation</note>, result in the following principal amounts <note>original: *mūla-dhanāni*</note>: 18, 24, and 27.
The equal profits <note>original: *sama-lābhāḥ*</note> are 36.

Now, in the second example, where the principal including profit <note>original: *salābha-mūla-dhana*</note> is mentioned: the principals including profit are 1/3, 2/5, and 3/7. By the process of distribution, the principal amounts are:

| 16 | 23 | 25 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 47 | 42 | 55 |
| 122 | 122 | 122 |

<note>This table represents fractional values: 16 47/122, 23 42/122, and 25 55/122</note> In the third example, where the principal excluding profit <note>original: *vilābha-mūla-dhana*</note> is mentioned, here is the illustration.

Furthermore:

> Six, seven, and eight are the purchase rates; five, six, and seven are the sales. If the mixed sum <note>original: *miśra*; the total value</note> is two hundred and seventeen, how is the equal principal excluding profit found?

**Statement:** 6/5, 7/6, 8/7. The mixed wealth <note>original: *miśra-dhana*</note> is 217.

Here, from the proportion based on the amount excluding profit, the principal amounts are found to be 75, 72, and 70.
The principal minus the profit is the equal wealth: 60.

### Rule

> * Divide the price by its own portion [quantity]; then the procedure for the respective commodities is as before. <note>Verse 2</note>

### Example

> For one *dramma* <note>a silver coin</note>, one *pala* <note>a unit of weight, approx. 40 grams</note> of ginger minus a quarter <note>3/4 pala</note>; for two *drammas*, one *pala* of asafoetida plus a third <note>4/3 pala</note>; for three *drammas*, one *pala* of long pepper plus a fifth <note>6/5 pala</note>. Tell me, how are these equal for ten *drammas*?

**Statement:** 1/1, 1/2, 1/3 <note>representing the drammas</note>. Total mixture: 10. The values for a unit quantity <note>original: *rūpa-sabhāga-maulyāni*</note> are 4/3, 3/2, and 5/2. <note>These are calculated by dividing the price by the quantity: 1 ÷ 3/4 = 4/3; 2 ÷ 4/3 = 3/2; 3 ÷ 6/5 = 5/2</note> Therefore, by the process of distribution as before, the prices for the ginger and the others are found to be 5/3, 25/8, and 125/24.

---

* "One should divide the prices by the goods," etc. This is exactly in accordance with what Bhaskara <note>a 12th-century Indian mathematician, author of the Lilavati</note> has stated.

50/13 and 80/13. Consequently, the measures of the sandalwood and saffron in *palas* are 100/13 and 200/13 <note>Note that 200 is double 100, satisfying the "double quantity" requirement</note>.

> **O learned one, if ninety-five pomegranate fruits <note>original: *śata-pañca-kona*; "one hundred less five"</note> are obtained for one *dramma* <note>a silver coin</note>, and five mango fruits <note>original: *sahakāra*; a fragrant variety of mango</note> are obtained for one *pana* <note>a copper coin; typically 16 *panas* equal 1 *dramma*, though rates varied</note>; tell me, by what number of *panas* can these fruits be obtained so that their total sum, when increased by seven, becomes one hundred?**

**Statement:** 12/95, 1/5. Mixed: 93 <note>The OCR reads 63, but the solution 36 + 57 = 93 confirms the total must be 93, satisfying the verse's condition: 93 + 7 = 100</note>. Here, the quantities of equal value are to be determined by proportion.

<term>त्रैराशिकम् (Trairāśika)</term> The "Rule of Three," a method to find an unknown value using three known values based on proportion.
<term>न्यासः (Nyāsa)</term> The "Statement" or "Layout," referring to how the numbers are arranged on the mathematician's board before calculation.
<term>क्रयविक्रयौ (Kraya-vikrayau)</term> Purchase and sale; the section of arithmetic dealing with trade.
<term>वृद्धिविधनम् (Vṛddhi-vidhana)</term> Rules for calculating interest (*vṛddhi*).
<term>कलान्तरं (Kalāntara)</term> Another term for interest, specifically the "difference" over time.
<term>मूलधनं (Mūladhana)</term> The principal or "root-wealth."
<term>मिश्रम् (Miśra)</term> The "mixed" amount, referring to the sum of principal and interest combined.
<term>हंसयूथमानम् (Haṃsa-yūtha-māna)</term> The measure or total count of a flock of swans.
<term>निष्क (Niṣka)</term> A high-value denomination, usually a gold coin or a specific weight of gold.
<term>प्रक्षेपकरण (Prakṣepa-karaṇa)</term> The process of "distribution" or "alligation," used to share a total sum among parts in a specific ratio.

> ***A wealthy man gave a sum of money to a priest in the first year. This sum, O learned one, was doubled [every year] by someone. Tell me, if after five years the total sum became one hundred and eighty-six, what was the initial amount given?***

**Rule**

### The desired [value] minus or plus the interest—
### divided by the desired, the [unknown] here becomes the result.
