<meta>This page concludes the "Hidden Treasure" problem and provides the algebraic justification for the "Rule of Dissimilar Bundles." It follows the previous page's calculation where a group of men found a treasure and compared its value to their individual wealth. This section formalizes the method for cases where each man's claim involves different multipliers and different portions of the treasure.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

> [One's own wealth] reduced by its own multiplier,
> and divided by that [multiplier] increased by one, the individual amounts of wealth result. 
> The sum of the others' wealth multiplied by one's own 
> multiplier, minus one's own [share of the treasure], should be divided. <note>Verse 31 provides the algorithmic steps to isolate an individual's wealth from the collective sum when a shared treasure is involved.</note> ३१ 
> From its own portion, the treasure 
> is produced; that which was previously unequal. 
> Having made the denominators equal and then discarding the denominators,
> those are the shares, just as before. ३२ 

---

-> $= (\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1) (\text{Wealth}_2 + \text{Wealth}_3 + \text{Wealth}_4)$ <-

Therefore: $\frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} + \frac{\text{Treasure Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} = \text{Wealth}_2 + \text{Wealth}_3 + \text{Wealth}_4 ...... (1)$

Similarly: $\frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{\text{Treasure Share}_2}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} = \text{First} + \text{Third} + \text{Fourth}$

$\frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{\text{Treasure Share}_3}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} = \text{First} + \text{Second} + \text{Fourth}$

$\frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} + \frac{\text{Treasure Share}_4}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} = \text{First} + \text{Second} + \text{Third}$

**By the total sum:**

$\text{Sum} \left( \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} + \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} \right)$  
$+ \text{Treasure} \left( \frac{\text{Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_2}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_3}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_4}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} \right)$  
$= (\text{n} - 1) \text{Sum}$ <note>The value "n" represents the number of men, in this case, 4. Subtracting 1 accounts for the fact that in each equation, one man's wealth is excluded from the sum of the others.</note>

$\therefore \text{Sum} \left\{ \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} + \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} - (\text{n} - 1) \right\}$

<note>The text concludes with the algebraic reduction required to find the value of the 'Sum' (Yoga) in terms of the 'Treasure' (Nidhi). This allows the mathematician to solve for all unknown variables once one 'chosen' value is assigned.</note>

<summary>This page presents Verses 31-32 of a mathematical treatise, detailing the algebraic derivation for solving problems involving a hidden treasure shared among several people with different multipliers. It uses a system of linear equations to show how to find the total sum and individual wealth by finding a common denominator and isolating variables.</summary>
<keywords>Bijaganita, Sanskrit mathematics, algebra, multipliers, hidden treasure problem, common denominator, systems of equations, Bhaskara</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page provides a concrete example of the "Hidden Treasure" problem introduced on the previous page. It demonstrates the algebraic derivation for finding individual wealth when each person's personal assets, combined with a specific fraction of a shared treasure, equal a multiple of the other participants' combined wealth. This type of problem is characteristic of the Bījanita (algebra) tradition.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>

### Example

> A treasure was obtained by four wealthy men.
> With its fifth, sixth, seventh, and fourth parts respectively [added to their own wealth],
> they each became two, three, four, and five times
> the sum of the others' wealth. <note>This verse sets up a system of linear equations where individual wealth is the unknown and the treasure's value is the shared constant.</note>

**Statement:** Multipliers: 2 [with share] $\frac{1}{7}$ | 3 [with share] $\frac{1}{6}$ | 4 [with share] $\frac{1}{5}$ | 5 [with share] $\frac{1}{4}$ <note>The layout pairs each multiplier with a specific fraction of the treasure as specified in the verse.</note>

**Procedure:** Here, the shares belonging to the others... are multiplied by one's own multiplier.

---

$= \text{Treasure} \times \left( \frac{\text{Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_2}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_3}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_4}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} \right)$

Then the total sum of all wealth is =

$$\text{Treasure} \times \left\{ \frac{\frac{\text{Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_2}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_3}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_4}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1}}{(n - 1) - \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1} - \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} - \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} - \frac{1}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1}} \right\}$$

By substituting this into Equation (1) and subtracting the sum of $(\text{Wealth}_2 + \text{Wealth}_3 + \text{Wealth}_4)$, the following value is obtained:

$$\text{Wealth}_1 = \frac{\text{Treasure}}{\text{Denominator}(\text{Multiplier}_1 + 1)} \left\{ \frac{\text{Share}_2 \cdot \text{Multiplier}_1 + \text{Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_2 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_3 \cdot \text{Multiplier}_1 + \text{Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_3 + 1} + \frac{\text{Share}_4 \cdot \text{Multiplier}_1 + \text{Share}_1}{\text{Multiplier}_4 + 1} - \text{Share}_1 (n - 2) \right\}$$

Then, following the statement and using the **inverse method**, the value of the treasure is determined. Here, to find the values of individual wealth, the <term>Teacher<note>original: "Acharya"; likely referring to Bhaskara II or a similar authority</note></term> first assumed the value of the treasure to be equal to the common denominator. Then, to ensure the results are integers (without fractions), those values are multiplied by an optional desired factor; thus, the wealth of the men can take many possible values. Everything is thus mathematically consistent.

<sig>12</sig>

**Nidhanam**: Treasure/Deposit.
**Dhanibhih**: By the wealthy men.
**Bhagaih**: By the parts or shares.
**Guna**: Multiplier.
**Nyasah**: Statement or mathematical setup.
**Karanam**: Calculation or procedure.
**Samikarane**: In the equation.
**Nidhimanam**: The measure/value of the treasure.
**Vyastavidhina**: By the inverse method.
**Acharyena**: By the Teacher.
**Ishtagunani**: Multiplied by a desired/chosen factor.
</vocab>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>( 60 )</header>

1/6 | 1/5 | 1/4 added to <term>one's own share</term> <note>original: "nijabhāga"</note> of 1/7 [gives] 20/42 | 16/35 | 15/28. These, divided by the <term>multipliers increased by unity</term> <note>original: "svarūpairguṇakaiḥ"; this refers to (n+1) where n is the multiplier</note> 4, 5, and 6, become 20/168 | 16/175 | 15/168.

The <term>sum</term> <note>original: "yogaḥ"</note> of these is 1406/4200. Multiplied by <term>one's own multiplier increased by unity</term> <note>original: "nijaguṇakena saikena"</note>, [which is] 3, it becomes 703/6300.

This is the <term>first wealth</term> <note>original: "prathamadhanam"</note>. Similarly, the wealth of the other three are 716/16800, 23/420, and 195/2016. The sum of the <term>others' wealth</term> <note>original: "paradhanayogaḥ"</note> is 487/3150. Multiplied by this multiplier 2, it becomes 487/1575.

Following the rule <term>"excluding one's own"</term> <note>original: "svasvavarjita"</note>, this is subtracted by one's own wealth 703/6300, [yielding] 41/140. Divided by this <term>fractional part</term> <note>original: "bhinnabhāgena"</note> 1/7, the <term>individual wealth</term> <note>original: "nijadhanam"</note> becomes 41/20. In this manner, separately calculating from the others' wealth, the <term>treasure</term> <note>original: "nidhānaṃ"</note> remains the same. To make these <term>non-fractional</term> <note>original: "abhinnārthaṃ"</note>, they are reduced to a <term>common denominator</term> <note>original: "samacchedī"</note>.

824/50400 | 2157/50400 | 2760/50400 | 2875/50400. And the treasure is 103320. By multiplying by an optional number to clear the denominators, the <term>integers</term> <note>original: "abhinnāni"</note> are produced: 824, 2157, 2760, and 2875. The treasure is 103320.

Alternatively, the <term>parts</term> <note>original: "athavāṃśāḥ"</note> 1/7 | 1/6 | 1/5 | 1/4, when their denominators are equalized and removed, are 60, 70, 84, 105. From these, the same wealth amounts should be calculated as before.

# Rule

> ->**The shares obtained, combined with the**<-
> ->**multipliers increased by unity, are multiplied as in the unequal bundle;**<-
> ->**their own multipliers, divided by the**<-
> ->**multipliers increased by unity, and their sum [is taken]. || 33 ||**<-

---

# Derivation

1 Here is the <term>demonstration</term> <note>original: "upapattiḥ"</note>. If, as before, $w_1, w_2, w_3, w_4$... are the values of wealth, and $m_1, m_2$... are the values of the multipliers, and $s_1, s'_1$ are the values of the first person's obtained share, $s_2, s'_2$ for the second, $s_3, s'_3$ for the third, then according to the statement of the problem:

->( 61 )<-

# Multiplied by the multiplier decreased by unity—
# their respective riches [are found] separately.

### Example.

> There are three men who are merchants. Among them, the first speaks with enthusiasm: 
> "If you two give me six and eight [of your coins], I will have twice the wealth of both of you." 
> The second says: "Give me eight and seven, and I shall be three times as wealthy as you." 
> The third says: "Seven and nine [given to me], and I shall have five times your wealth." 
> Tell their individual riches, if you possess any pride in calculation!

**Statement.** Multipliers: $\begin{matrix} 2 & 3 & 5 \\ 6 & 8 & 7 \\ 8 & 7 & 8 \end{matrix}$ <note>These numbers represent the multipliers and the specific amounts requested from the other two merchants respectively.</note> Here is the **method** <note>original: "karaṇam"</note>. The product of the sum of the wealth received by oneself:

The sums received are 14, 15, and 16. The multipliers increased by one are 3, 4, and 6.

---

$W_1 + R_2 + R_3 = M_1 \{ W_2 + W_3 - ( R_2 + R_3 ) \}$ <note>Here, W represents individual wealth, R represents the amount received from others, and M is the multiplier.</note>
By **simplification** <note>original: "samashodhanena"; literally "by equal-clearing," a standard term for algebraic reduction</note>, $W_1 + ( M_1 + 1 ) ( R_2 + R_3 ) = M_1 ( W_2 + W_3 )$
Or, $W_1 + W_2 + W_3 + ( M_1 + 1 ) ( R_2 + R_3 ) = ( M_1 + 1 ) ( W_2 + W_3 )$
$\therefore \frac{S}{M_1 + 1} + \frac{( M_1 + 1 ) ( R_2 + R_3 )}{M_1 + 1} = W_2 + W_3$ <note>S represents the total sum of all wealth.</note>
Therefore, if in the **demonstration** <note>original: "upapatti"</note> of the previous rule, we assume $n = 1$, $( M_1 + 1 ) ( R_2 + R_3 ) = D_1$, and $( M_2 + 1 ) ( R_3 + R_1 ) = D_2$, and so on, then:
The measure of wealth—
$W_1 = \frac{1}{Div} \times \frac{1}{M_1 + 1} \{ \frac{D_2 \cdot M_1 + D_1}{M_2 + 1} + \frac{D_3 \cdot M_1 + D_1}{M_3 + 1} - D_1 ( n - 2 ) \}$
Now, the previously established **divisor** <note>original: "hara"</note> is $Div = ( n - 1 ) - \frac{1}{M_1 + 1} - \frac{1}{M_2 + 1} - \frac{1}{M_3 + 1}$
$= 1 - \frac{1}{M_1 + 1} + 1 - \frac{1}{M_2 + 1} + 1 - \frac{1}{M_3 + 1} - 1$
$= \frac{M_1}{M_1 + 1} + \frac{M_2}{M_2 + 1} + \frac{M_3}{M_3 + 1} - 1$. Here, everything is demonstrated.

**Merchants** <note>original: "vaṇijaḥ"</note>, **Wealth** <note>original: "dhanam"</note>, **Multipliers** <note>original: "guṇāḥ"</note>, **Statement/Layout** <note>original: "nyāsaḥ"</note>, **Sum of received [amounts]** <note>original: "prāptayogaḥ"</note>, **By simplification** <note>original: "samashodhanena"</note>, **Demonstration/Proof** <note>original: "upapatti"</note>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical resolution of systems of equations. While the previous page dealt with the specific problem of three merchants sharing their wealth, this page transitions into the general proof and a new rule (Sutra) concerning the purchase of "living beings" (likely birds or livestock) at varying prices, a classic problem in Indian mathematics known as the Kapotādi-vikraya.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

Multiplied: 42 | 60 | 66. From these, the riches bundled together <note>original: "potalavaddha"; refers to grouped or intermediate values in the calculation</note> are:
$\frac{25}{2} | \frac{15}{1} | \frac{35}{12}$ <note>The value 35/12 appears to be a scribal error or OCR artifact for 35/2, which would correctly yield the final result of 14.</note>

Multipliers: 2 | 3 | 5. The multipliers increased by one: 3 | 4 | 6. 
Dividing [the multipliers] by these: $\frac{2}{3} | \frac{3}{4} | \frac{5}{6}$.

The sum of these is $\frac{9}{4}$ <note>The transcription says 8/4, but the math for 2/3 + 3/4 + 5/6 equals 27/12, which simplifies to 9/4.</note>. This sum decreased by unity <note>original: "rūponena"; subtracting the number 1</note> is $\frac{5}{4}$. 

The previous riches $\frac{25}{2} | \frac{15}{1} | \frac{35}{12}$ divided [by 5/4] results in the final riches: 10 | 12 | 14.

### Rule <note>original: "Sūtram"</note>

->**The [number of] living beings multiplied by the highest single price,**<-
->**and decreased by their own respective values;**<-
->**The remainders should be multiplied by desired multipliers**<-
->**according to one's own intelligence, as one wishes, to make them equal.**<-
->**The price of the living being multiplied by the multiplier,**<-
->**minus the divisor... the multiplier is determined.**<-

---

1. Here is the **demonstration** <note>original: "upapatti"; the logical proof or derivation of the rule</note>.
Here, the birds <note>original: "pakṣiṇaḥ"</note> are represented sequentially as $P_1, P_2, P_3...$
Their prices <note>original: "mūlyāni"</note> are $M_1, M_2, M_3...$
The sum of the desired birds is abbreviated as $Payo$ <note>original: "payo," short for "pakṣi-yoga"</note>.
The sum of the desired coins <note>original: "drammāḥ," a historical currency</note> is $Dvayo$ <note>original: "dvayo," short for "dramma-yoga"</note>.
The multipliers for the prices of the living beings are sequentially $G_1, G_2, G_3...$

Then, according to the statement of the problem:

$\begin{cases} P_1 \cdot G_1 + P_2 \cdot G_2 + P_3 \cdot G_3 + P_4 \cdot G_4 = \text{Payo} \\ M_1 \cdot G_1 + M_2 \cdot G_2 + M_3 \cdot G_3 + M_4 \cdot G_4 = \text{Dvayo} \end{cases}$

In this case, if the price of a single bird of the fourth kind is: $\frac{M_4}{P_4} = M$ (where $M$ is the highest of all prices), then by multiplying the first equation [by $M$], we obtain:

$P_1 \cdot G_1 \cdot M + P_2 \cdot G_2 \cdot M + P_3 \cdot G_3 \cdot M + M_4 \cdot G_4 = M \cdot \text{Payo} \cdots\cdots (1)$
$M_1 \cdot G_1 + M_2 \cdot G_2 + M_3 \cdot G_3 + M_4 \cdot G_4 = \text{Dvayo} \cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots (2)$

Subtracting the second equation from the first, we get:
$G_1(M - P_1 \cdot M) + G_2(M - P_2 \cdot M) + G_3(M - P_3 \cdot M) + 0$
$= \text{Dvayo} - M \cdot \text{Payo} \cdots\cdots (3)$

# Example

> Five swans are obtained for three [drammas]; seven lazy donkeys <note>The text uses "khara" (donkey), though the final line refers to a "hundred birds" (khaga-shatam). In mathematical puzzles of this era, animals were often grouped loosely, or this refers to a specific bird like a heron.</note> for five; seven noisy parrots for nine; and peacocks for nine [drammas] are found in the market street. Also, three softly warbling cuckoos are obtained for nine [drammas] for the pleasure of the king. O learned one, having taken these, bring me exactly one hundred birds for one hundred <term>drammas</term> <note>A silver coin used in medieval India, derived from the Greek "drachma".</note>.

**Calculation:**
| 3 | 5 | 7 | 9 | 100 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 5 | 7 | 9 | 3 | 100 |

Here, the highest price of a single living being is 3 <note>Calculated by dividing the price by the quantity: 9/3 = 3. This is the highest unit price among the groups.</note>. The quantities of beings are multiplied by this: 15, 21, 27, 9, 300. Subtracting their respective prices: 12, 16, 20, 0, 200. These values are reduced by four <note>Divided by the common factor of 4 to simplify the equation.</note>:
3, 4, 5, 0, 50. From these, the **pulverizers** <note>original: "kuṭṭakāḥ"; the values used in the Pulverizer method to solve indeterminate linear equations.</note> are produced: 1, 8, 3; or 1, 3, 7; or 2, 4, 6; or 3, 6, 1; or 4, 2, 6; or 4, 7, 2; or 5, 5, 3; or 6, 3, 4; or 7, 1, 15; or 7, 6, 1; or 8, 4, 2; or 6, 2, 3; or 11, 3, 1; or 12, 11, 1. By multiplying the specified prices or the beings by these, or by subtracting from the total wealth or beings, and dividing the remainder by the **wealth in the pulverizer's position** or the beings, the **multiplier for the pulverizer's position** is produced—

| 4 | 11 | 11 | 4 | 4 | 13 | 4 | 13 | 13 | 13 | 14 | 14 | 6 | 5 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 3 | 3 | 1 | 1 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 3 | 3 | 3 | 1 | 5 | 1 |

| 4 |
|---|
| 1 |

Thus, in the first pulverizer, the **multipliers** produced are 1, 8, 3, 4. When multiplied by these, the **prices and beings** are:

**Price/Beings**
| 3 | 40 | 21 | 36 |
|---|----|----|----|
| 5 | 56 | 27 | 12 |

Thus, there are many such solutions.

---

( 3 ) In this [process], having applied reduction where possible, one should assume desired values for $m_1, m_2, m_3$ <note>original: "gu₁", "gu₂", "gu₃"; representing the multipliers (gunaka).</note> such that both sides of the equation are equal. Then, by substituting the values of the known multipliers into the first two equations, the value of the unknown multiplier becomes known. Here, "the highest unit price" is mentioned as a general indicator; therefore, one may perform the operation by assuming the price of any desired being as the multiplier. That same multiplier then becomes clear through the final operation.

**Example** (udāharaṇa): A practical problem to demonstrate a rule.
**Pulverizer** (kuṭṭaka): An algebraic method for solving indeterminate equations of the form ax + c = by.
**Multiplier** (guṇaka): The coefficient or factor used in the calculation.
**Price** (mūlya): The cost value.
**Living being** (jīva): The items being purchased (birds/animals).
**Reduction** (apavartana): Simplifying a ratio or equation by dividing by a common factor.
**Equation** (samīkaraṇa): The statement of equality between two mathematical expressions.
</vocab>

<meta>This page transitions from the bird-buying puzzles of the previous page to a classic "cistern problem" (filling a tank with multiple sources) and begins the formal algebraic proof for the complex commercial problems discussed earlier.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

### Rule

-> Unity divided by the sum of unit fractions <-
-> is the time of completion. <-

### Example

> If several springs fill a cistern <note>original: "vāpī"; a stepped well or water reservoir common in ancient India.</note> individually in one day, half a day, a third, and a quarter of a day; tell me, in what fraction of a day will it be filled if they are all released together?

**Statement of the problem:**
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 |
The result, the time to fill the cistern, in fractions <note>original: "bhāga"; a part or fraction.</note> of a day:
| 1 |
| :---: |
| 10 | 

### Rule

-> The greater wealth joined with the desired quantity <-
-> the sale measure indeed multiplied by that. [Verse 36] <-

---

<margin>1</margin> "Divide unity <note>The number 1.</note> by the sum of the fractions of the denominators; that shall be the time of completion." <note>original: "bhajecchido'ṃśairatha tairvimiśrai rūpaṃ bhavet syāt paripūrttikālaḥ"</note> This rule is consistent with the one stated by Bhāskara <note>Referring to Bhāskara II, the 12th-century author of the famous mathematical treatise *Līlāvatī*.</note>.

<margin>2</margin> Here is the proof <term>upapatti</term> <note>In Indian mathematics, this refers to a logical demonstration or "derivation" of why a rule works.</note>. Let the purchase measure = *Pur*; the sale measure = *Sal*; the remaining sale measure = *Rem*. Let the principal wealth amounts be respectively $W_1, W_2, W_3, W_4$, where $W_4$ is the greatest. In the first sale, the quantities obtained are respectively $X_1, X_2, X_3, X_4$, and the multiplier for the principal wealth = $M$. Then, according to the question, the first profit is:
Profit = $Rem \times (Pur \times W_1 - Sal \times X_1) + X_1 - (M \times W_1)$

| | |
|---|---|
| Thus, | = $W_1 \times (Pur \times Rem - M) - X_1 \times (Sal \times Rem - 1)$ |
| | = $W_2 \times (Pur \times Rem - M) - X_2 \times (Sal \times Rem - 1)$ |
| | = $W_3 \times (Pur \times Rem - M) - X_3 \times (Sal \times Rem - 1)$ |
| | = $W_4 \times (Pur \times Rem - M) - X_4 \times (Sal \times Rem - 1)$ |

Here, the common divisor <term>samāpavartana</term> <note>A factor used to reduce numbers to their simplest form.</note> of $W_1, W_2$, etc. = $S$. By reducing with that divisor, the wealth values become $W'_1, W'_2$, and so on. Then:

$W'_1 \times (Pur \times Rem - M) - X_1 \times (\frac{Sal \times Rem - 1}{S}) = W'_2 \times (Pur \times Rem - M)$

<meta>This page continues the formal algebraic proof (upapatti) started on page 64. It specifically derives the formula for the "Purchase Measure" and demonstrates that the profit remains constant for all individuals in the problem, regardless of their initial wealth, provided the variables are set according to these rules.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>

## The purchase measure is the sum of the remainder and the multiplier, minus one.

---

$- X_2 \left( \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right)$

$= Wealth^1_1 ( Purchase \cdot Remainder - Multiplier ) - X_2 \left( \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right)$

$= Wealth^1_4 ( Purchase \cdot Remainder - Multiplier ) - X_4 \left( \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right) = Profit$

In this context, if $Purchase = \frac{Sale ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - 1}{Base}$, then:

$X_1 = Wealth^1_1 ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - 1$ if $Sale > Wealth^1_1$

$X_2 = Wealth^1_2 ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - 1$ if $Sale > Wealth^1_2$

$X_3 = Wealth^1_3 ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - 1$ if $Sale > Wealth^1_3$

$X_4 = Wealth^1_4 ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - 1$ if $Sale > Wealth^1_4$

And $Profit = Wealth^1_1 ( Purchase \cdot Remainder - Multiplier ) - X_1 \left( \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right)$

$= Wealth^1_1 \left\{ \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - Remainder - Base \cdot Multiplier}{Base} \right\}$

$- \left\{ Wealth^1_1 ( Remainder + Multiplier \cdot Base ) - 1 \right\} \left\{ \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right\}$

$= Wealth^1_1 ( Remainder + Base \cdot Multiplier ) \left( \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right)$

$- \left\{ Wealth^1_1 ( Remainder + Base \cdot Multiplier ) - 1 \right\} \left\{ \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \right\}$

$= \frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base} \text{.}$ <note>The final result of the subtraction simplifies to this expression.</note> This measure of profit is the same everywhere <note>Meaning it is constant for all parties involved.</note>.

Therefore, the value of the Sale Measure should be assumed in such a way that the expression $\frac{Sale \cdot Remainder - 1}{Base}$ results in an integer <note>original: "idambhinna"; literally "unbroken" or non-fractional.</note>, and also...

<margin>
**Vocabulary used in this section:**
*Remainder-part-multiplier-sum* <note>original: "śeṣārdhaguṇakayogo"</note>, *minus unity* <note>original: "rūpavihīnaḥ"</note>, *purchase* <note>original: "krayo"</note>, *measure of profit* <note>original: "lābhamānaṃ"</note>, *this is an integer* <note>original: "idambhinnamatha"</note>, *unknown quantity* <note>original: "yāvat-tāvat"; the standard term for a variable like 'X' in Indian algebra</note>, *multiplier* <note>original: "guṇaka"</note>, *sale* <note>original: "vikraya"</note>, *purchase* <note>original: "kraya"</note>.
</margin>

<summary>This page provides the algebraic derivation to prove that profit remains uniform across different scenarios in a complex commercial problem. It concludes by establishing that the purchase price must be calculated using a specific ratio to ensure the results are whole numbers.</summary>
<keywords>algebraic proof, purchase price, profit constant, Yāvat-tāvat, integer solutions, commercial arithmetic, Indian mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of a mathematical problem involving equal profits among four individuals with different initial capital. It moves from the algebraic derivation seen on page 45 to a practical example involving the purchase of plantains and introduces a commentary comparing the standard method of the "Acharya" (the original master, likely Bhaskara II) with a refined method by the commentator.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>( 66 )</header>

### Example

> Having purchased plantain fruits with six, ten, fifty, and one hundred *panas* <note>original: *pana*—a copper coin used as a standard unit of currency</note>, 
> and having sold them at a uniform price, and then selling the remainder at a rate of six per fruit, 
> the final amounts, including profit, became equal for all, O friend. Tell me, what was the purchase and what was the sale price? 
> Are they equal when the respective original capitals are subtracted, or when twice the original capitals are subtracted? 
> If you have reached the peak of the mountain of pride in calculation <note>original: *ganaka*—a calculator or mathematician</note>, then speak!

**The setup for the first case:** Capital values: 6, 10, 50, 100. Price of the remainder: 6. Multiplier: 0. 
In this case, with a chosen value <note>original: *ista*—an assumed or arbitrary value used to solve the equation</note> of 1, the Sale becomes 101 and the Purchase becomes 605. With a chosen value of 2, the Sale becomes 102 and the Purchase becomes 611.

**The setup for the second example:** Capital values: 6, 10, 50, 100. Price of the remainder: 6. Multiplier: 1. 
With a chosen value of 1, the Sale and Purchase are 101 and 706. With a chosen value of 2, the Sale and Purchase are 102 and 713.

**The setup for the third example:** Capital values: 6, 10, 50, 100. Price of the remainder: 6. Multiplier: 2. 
With a chosen value of 1, the Sale and Purchase are 102 and 807. With a chosen value of 2, the Sale and Purchase are 100 and 815. Thus, by the power of choice, there are infinite solutions.

---

**Method 4:** If the Remainder is the dividend <note>original: *bhajya*</note>, a negative unit is the additive <note>original: *kshepa*</note>, and the Common Divisor <note>original: *samapavartana*</note> is the divisor, then by the **Pulverizer method** <note>original: *kuttaka-vidhi*—an algorithm for solving linear Diophantine equations of the form ax + b = cy</note>, there are many values for the multiplier. The Acharya, having assumed the Common Divisor to be 1, determined the measures of sale and purchase without using the Pulverizer. In that case, according to **Method 4**:
Purchase = Sale (Remainder + Multiplier) - 1.

**By my method:** 
Purchase = $\frac{\text{Sale ( Remainder + Multiplier $\cdot$ Common Divisor ) - 1}}{\text{Common Divisor}}$ = $\frac{\text{Sale $\cdot$ Remainder - 1}}{\text{Common Divisor}}$ + Sale $\cdot$ Multiplier

This means that in the Pulverizer method, the sale value is the quotient, and the purchase value is proven to be the product of the multiplier and the sale value added to the result.

However, where the Remainder and the Common Divisor are not relatively prime <note>original: *drdha*—literally "firm," meaning they share a factor</note>, even in my method, the Common Divisor must be assumed to be 1. For example, in the Acharya's own illustration, the Common Divisor of the wealth amounts is 2, and the price of the remainder is 6. Since these are not relatively prime, the Common Divisor here must be taken as 1. If the price of the remainder were 5 in the Acharya's example, then by my method in the third example:

Dividend 5, Additive 1
---------------------- = {Quotient = 2, Multiplier = 1} using 25 as the chosen value.
Divisor 2

<summary>This page provides a practical example of a profit-equalization problem involving plantains and compares two mathematical approaches: the Acharya's simplified formula and the commentator's more generalized Pulverizer (Kuttaka) method.</summary>
<keywords>Kuttaka, Pulverizer method, plantains, Pana, Bhajya, Kshepa, arithmetic, Diophantine equations, Indian mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical investigation into equalizing wealth among multiple parties. While the previous page dealt with whole-number capitals (6, 10, 50, 100), this page introduces a new rule for handling fractional capital values using common denominators.</meta>

<page-num>( 97 )</page-num>

### Rule

> **For capitals made to have equal denominators,**
> **the sale and purchase are as before once the denominators are removed. || 37 ||**
> **Having brought them to a common denominator,**
> **the product of the purchase and the denominator is the purchase for the fractions.**

### Example

> **Having bought plantain fruits with a half, a third, a fifth, and a quarter <note>original: *ardha-tribhāga-pañcama-caraṇa*—representing the fractions 1/2, 1/3, 1/5, and 1/4</note>,**
> **and having sold them at a uniform rate, and then selling the remainder at four for each here—**
> **they became equal in wealth. O learned one, tell me the sale and purchase rates.**

The setup:
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 3 | 5 | 4 |
The price of the remainder is 4. Here is the working. Making the 

denominators equal:
| 30 | 20 | 12 | 15 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 60 | 60 | 60 | 60 |
Once the denominators are removed, we get: 30, 20, 12, 15.

As before, using an assumed value <note>original: *ishta*—a chosen constant to solve the indeterminate equation</note> of 1, the Sale and Purchase rates are 31 and 123. This purchase rate, 123, is multiplied by the common denominator of 60, resulting in 7,380 as the purchase rate for the fractional capitals. Thus, the Sale is 1,860 <note>calculated as 31 × 60</note>. With an assumed value of 2, the Sale and Purchase rates are 32 and 7,620.

->Thus, there are infinite solutions based on the assumed value.<-

---

Multiplier = 51. Quotient = 127. Therefore, the Sale = 51.
Purchase = Quotient + (Sale × 2) = 127 + 2 $\times$ 51 = 127 + 102 = 229.
According to the Teacher's <note>original: *Acharya*—referring to the traditional method of the master mathematician, likely Bhaskara II</note> method, by choosing 1 as the assumed value, the Sale = 101, and the Purchase = 101(5 + 2) - 1 = 706. Following the Teacher’s method results in very large numbers for the purchase and sale.

<margin>1</margin> Here is the proof: It is very easy to understand because, for those capitals with equal denominators, if one uses the numerators as the wealth and calculates the sale and purchase rates as before, and if the purchase rate is then multiplied by the common denominator, the resulting quantity will be equal to the previous product of the purchase and the numerator. In this case, the sale rate remains exactly as previously calculated.

<sig>13</sig>

<vocab>Rule (*sūtram*), Example (*udāharaṇam*), Setup (*nyāsaḥ*), Common Denominator (*samaccheda*), Purchase (*kraya*), Sale (*vikraya*), Proof/Derivation (*upapatti*)</vocab>

<summary>This page provides a rule for solving the "equal wealth" problem when the initial capitals are fractions (1/2, 1/3, 1/4, 1/5). It demonstrates how to use common denominators to convert the problem into integers and notes that the author's method yields smaller, more manageable numbers than the traditional method of the Acharya.</summary>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical investigation into the "Equal Wealth" problem. Following the previous page's focus on converting fractional capitals into common denominators, this page provides a formal rule (Sutra 38) and its algebraic derivation (Upapatti) based on historical commentaries.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>

### Rule

> **When divided by the divisors <note>denominators</note> through which it reaches a cleared state, that cleared value—combined with a chosen number and multiplied by the final sale price, then diminished by one—results in equal wealth. This occurs when multiplied by the final divisor. || 38 ||**
> **When multiplied by the cleared value, the purchase rate is certainly determined, whether the wealth is expressed in fractions or in whole numbers.**

---

### Proof

Here is the derivation. According to the method of **Krishna Daivajna** <note>A renowned 16th-century astronomer and mathematician who commented on Bhaskara II's work</note> (which can be seen in the notes on Bhaskara’s *Algebra* <note>original: *Bhāskara-bīja-ṭippaṇī*</note> by Shri Janaka, page 133):

To obtain an integer <note>original: *abhinna*—literally "unbroken" or non-fractional</note> value, one should determine a common denominator <term>samahāra <note>a shared divisor used to simplify fractions</note></term> by finding the least common multiple of the various denominators. Having added any desired value <term>iṣṭa <note>an arbitrary constant chosen to solve the indeterminate equation</note></term> to that, let the sale rate be the product of the "cleared" divisor and the final sale rate—expressed as: { (cleared value + chosen value) $\times$ $k$ }.

Then, having calculated the purchase rate as:
$\frac{a}{k}$ { (cleared value + chosen value) $\times$ $k$ } — 1 

To ensure the result is an integer, multiply this by the common denominator (designated as "cleared" or *śuddha*). Thus, the purchase rate is:
= $k \cdot \text{cleared value}$ [ $\frac{a}{k}$ { (cleared value + chosen value) $\times$ $k$ } — 1 ] = $k \cdot \text{cleared value} \cdot \text{Equal Wealth}$.

Because the measure of Equal Wealth = $\frac{a}{k}$ { (cleared value + chosen value) $\times$ $k$ } — 1.

In this context, the remaining sale rate is $\frac{a}{k}$. The intelligent scholar should understand that the "remainder sale" in Krishna Daivajna's method is the reciprocal, $\frac{k}{a}$. This alternative method applies universally to both fractional and whole-number problems; thus, everything is proven. It is from this very method that Krishna Daivajna’s specific technique arises. To understand that derivation, one should consult the aforementioned page 133 of the *Algebra of Bhaskara* <note>original: *Bhāskara-bīja-gaṇita*</note> edited by Shri Janaka. In that context, the reduction factor <term>samāpavarttana</term> should be assumed as one <note>unity</note>.

<summary>This page defines a generalized rule for solving "equal wealth" problems using indeterminate equations and provides a technical proof based on the 16th-century algebraic methods of Krishna Daivajna.</summary>
<keywords>Krishna Daivajna, Bhaskara II, Algebra, common denominator, equal wealth, indeterminate equations, purchase rate, Sanskrit mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract mathematical rule and derivation provided on the previous page (Rule 38) to practical applications. It provides two specific examples involving the trade of goods divided into fractional shares to demonstrate how to calculate uniform purchase and sale rates that result in equal wealth.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

# Example

> **Having taken plantains <note>original: *rambhā-phalāni*; the fruit of the banana tree</note> in shares of one-half, one-third, one-quarter, and one-fifth, and having sold them at a uniform rate; then, the remainder being sold at a rate of one and a quarter <note>original: *aṅghri-sahita-paṇa*—literally, a *paṇa* (coin) increased by a foot (quarter), or 5/4</note> per unit—what are the purchase and sale rates here that would make the proceeds equal?**

**Layout:** $\frac{1}{2}$ ; $\frac{1}{3}$ ; $\frac{1}{4}$ ; $\frac{1}{5}$. The residual rate <note>original: *śeṣārgha*</note> is $\frac{5}{4}$.

**Here is the method:** A "chosen number" <term>iṣṭa-rāśi <note>an arbitrary value chosen to solve an indeterminate equation</note></term>, known here as the "cleared value" <note>original: *śuddhākhya*; likely referring to the least common multiple</note>, is set at 60 because it is divisible by all the denominators.
An arbitrary value of 1 is chosen. Added <note>to the cleared value</note>, it becomes 61. According to the rule "multiplied by the final sale divisor"—the final sale rate is $\frac{5}{4}$, so multiplying by its divisor <note>denominator</note> 4, the **sale rate is 244**.

Now, this 244 is multiplied by the final rate $\frac{5}{4}$, resulting in 305. Minus one <note>original: *nireka*</note>, it becomes 304. This is the **Equal Wealth amount: 304**.
When this is multiplied by the cleared value (60) and the final sale divisor (4), the **purchase rate is 72,960**.

Using 2 as the chosen arbitrary number, the purchase and sale rates are **74,160 and 248**.
Using 3, the purchase and sale rates are **75,360 and 252**.

---

->Furthermore:<-

> **Where the numerators <note>original: *lavāḥ*</note> are one, two, three, and four, and the denominators <note>original: *chedakāḥ*</note> follow starting from two <note>i.e., 2, 3, 4, and 5</note>, the wealth of four friends is equal, my friend. What would be the equal purchase and sale rates in that case? The final rates are equal to nine-eighths.**

**Layout:** $\frac{1}{2}$ ; $\frac{2}{3}$ ; $\frac{3}{4}$ ; $\frac{4}{5}$. The residual rate is $\frac{9}{8}$.
In this case, with 1 as the chosen arbitrary number, the purchase and sale rates are **263,040 and 448**.
With 2 as the chosen number, they are **267,360 and 456**.

<meta>This page shifts from the previous discussion on fractional shares and commodity trading to problems of relative motion. It introduces a rule for calculating when two travelers moving toward each other—and then reversing course—will meet. This reflects a transition into the "motion" (gati) section of the mathematical treatise.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

### The Rule

->**When the distance is divided by the sum of the speeds,**<-
->**the time of the first meeting is produced.**<-
->**When that distance is doubled,**<-
->**the time for the next meeting is found from that sum.**<-

### Example

->**The path between two certain cities is three hundred leagues <term>yojana <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles</note></term>.**<-
->**One traveler moves at a speed of eleven, and the other goes at nine leagues per day.**<-
->**Two couriers <note>original: *lipivāhaka*; literally "letter-carriers"</note> set out simultaneously from their own cities.**<-
->**Tell me the two times they meet: once as they go, and again as they return.**<-
->**O learned one, if you know, tell me quickly the time in single days.**<-

---

Let the speed of the first = $s_1$. The speed of the second = $s_2$. The distance between the cities = $d$.
If the sum of the speeds covers the distance in one day, then what time is obtained for the total distance?
The **first meeting time** = $\frac{d}{s_1 + s_2}$.

The distance traveled by the first in this time = $\frac{s_1 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$.
The distance traveled by the second = $\frac{s_2 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$.

Now, if the time until the second meeting after the first meeting is assumed to be '$x$' <note>original: *yā*; an abbreviation for *yāvattāvat*, the standard Sanskrit algebraic variable for an unknown</note>, then in this much time:
The travel of the first = $s_1 \cdot x$. Subtracting the distance already covered by the second, $\frac{s_2 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$, we get the first traveler's return journey distance: $s_1 \cdot x - \frac{s_2 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$.

Similarly, the second traveler's return journey distance = $s_2 \cdot x - \frac{s_1 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$.
The sum of both their return journeys must equal the total distance between the cities <note>because to meet a second time after reaching the opposite ends, their combined additional travel must equal twice the distance, or looking at it from the first meeting point, they must collectively cover the remaining gaps and the return distance</note>.

Therefore: $s_1 \cdot x - \frac{s_2 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2} + s_2 \cdot x - \frac{s_1 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$
$= (s_1 + s_2) \cdot x - d = d$.
Therefore, $x = \frac{2 \cdot d}{s_1 + s_2}$.
Thus, the rule is proven.

<vocab>Rule (sūtra), Example (udāharaṇa), League (yojana), Distance between cities (purāntara), Meeting time (samāgama-kāla), Travel/Motion (gamana), Return journey (parāvartana-gamana)</vocab>

<page-num>101</page-num>

### Statement
**The measure of leagues between the two cities is 300. The fixed speeds are 11 and 9 <note>The original text contains a scribal or OCR error here, but the values 11 and 9 are established on the previous page</note>. The result for the first meeting: 15 days. From the first meeting to the second meeting: 30 days.**

Also:

->One courier travels a fixed nine leagues<-
->every day, and the other travels five.<-
->The path to be traversed is two hundred and ten leagues.<-
->Tell me, friend, in how many days will their meeting occur?<-

### Statement
The two speeds are 9 <note>original: *8*; likely a transcription error as the verse above specifies *9*</note> and 5. The path to be traversed is 210.
<insert>For those setting out from the same city, the time of the meeting—specifically the second meeting—is 30 days.</insert>

### The Rule

->**The time of meeting on a circular path**<-
->**occurs when the circumference is divided by the difference in speeds.**<-

### Example

->**One travels eight leagues, and the other five.**<-
->**The circumference of the circular region is one hundred and twenty.**<-

---

<insert>For two moving simultaneously from one place with speeds respectively Speed 1 and Speed 2, where Speed 1 < Speed 2, let the distance between cities be *d*. Then the first meeting is at that time. If the time of the second meeting is assumed to be 'x' <note>original: *yā*, abbreviation for *yāvattāvat*, the unknown variable</note>, then (Speed 2 × x) - *d* is the travel on the second lap. (Speed 1 × x) is the first journey from its own place. The sum of these:
x(Speed 1 + Speed 2) - d = d.

Therefore, x = 
   2d
---------
Speed 1 + Speed 2
. This is proven.</insert>

1. The proof here: Let the circumference around the city be *C*. The daily speeds are respectively Speed 1 and Speed 2. Then the proportion: if one day corresponds to the difference in speeds, then what time is obtained for the total circumference?
Meeting time = 
     C
-----------
 Speed 1 ~ Speed 2 <note>The symbol ~ denotes the difference between the two values</note>
. This is proven.

<meta>This page concludes the problem of circular motion from the previous discussion and introduces a new mathematical rule concerning transport costs for goods that diminish (leak) during a journey.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

->( 103 )<-

And as those two travelers proceed along their own path, tell me: in how many days will they both reach their own city simultaneously?

**Statement of values:** Fixed speeds are 8 and 5. The total circumference <note>original: *paridhi*</note> of the region is 120. The time taken for them to simultaneously reach their own city is 40 days.

### The Rule

->**The transport fee for a leaking vessel**<-
->**is based on the remainder plus half of the loss.**<-

The transport fee <note>original: *bhāṭaka*, referring to the rental or carriage cost</note> should be determined by applying the Rule of Three <term>trairāśika—a mathematical method using three known values to find a fourth</term> to the remaining substance plus half of the measure of the leaked material.

---

1. If two travelers set out simultaneously from their own city and wander along the circumference, in how many days will they reach their city again at the same time?

2. Here is the mathematical proof: First, let the initial measure of the substance in the vessel be represented as *Mass*. At the end of the journey, the remaining measure is *Remainder*. Therefore, the amount leaked <note>original: *kṣaya*</note> is *Mass* minus *Remainder*. Let the distance of the path be *Distance*, and the total transport fee be *Fee*. Now, if the distance is the horizontal side and the quantity of the substance is the vertical side, 
<margin>
G ┌───┐
  │ \ │ Gh
A └───┘ K
<note>In this geometric proof, 'A' and 'K' represent the distance traveled, while 'A' to 'G' represents the initial quantity of goods.</note>
</margin>
then the transport fee is represented by the area of the work performed. This work is visualized as the area of the trapezoid A-K-Gh-G. Here, K-Gh is the measure of the remaining substance. The area of this shape is:

Area = $\frac{Distance \times (Initial Mass + Remainder)}{2}$

Which is the same as: $\frac{Distance \times (Leak + Remainder + Remainder)}{2}$

Which simplifies to: $\frac{Distance \times (Leak + 2 \times Remainder)}{2}$

Or: $Distance \times (\frac{Leak}{2} + Remainder)$

Therefore, by proportion: if the full transport fee <note>original: *bhāṭaka*</note> is earned for the work of carrying the full *Mass* over the whole *Distance*, then what fee is earned for the work represented by $Distance \times (\frac{Leak}{2} + Remainder)$? 

The result is: $\frac{Fee \times (\frac{Leak}{2} + Remainder)}{Mass}$

Thus, the rule is proven.

<vocab>Statement, Rule, Proof, Transport Fee, Rule of Three, Circumference, Leaked, Vessel</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from the previous page regarding the calculation of transport fees for goods that leak or diminish during transit. <page-num>103</page-num>

# Example

> If a vessel <term>original: *kutapa*; a measure for liquids</term> filled with three hundred *palas* <note>a unit of weight approximately 40-50 grams</note> of oil is carried for ten *yojanas* <note>a distance of roughly 8-9 miles</note>,
> And the set transport fee for that journey is nine *paṇas* <note>a copper coin</note>, but the vessel leaked through a hole;
> If only sixty *palas* remain at the end, tell me, O calculator, what fee should be paid?

**Statement of values:**
Distance: 10. Initial quantity: 300. Full fee: 9.
Remainder: 60. Leaked amount <note>original: *skanna*</note>: 240.
Half of the leaked oil: 120.
The remainder (60) combined with half the loss (120) equals 180.
The fee for this is calculated using the Rule of Five <term>pañcarāśika; a mathematical proportion using five known terms to find a sixth</term>—

| 10 | 10 | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 300 | 180 | The resulting fee is 5 <gloss>2</gloss>/<gloss>5</gloss> *paṇas*. |
| 9 | | |

# Procedural Rule for Intermediate Fees

> The goods multiplied by the measure of the distance,
> Divided by the distance to be traveled, is the "sum."
> The "sum" minus the goods, multiplied by the [distance covered],
> Results in the measure of the fee.
> From the difference of the sum and the product,
> Through that calculation, the lesser fee is found.

# Example

> If for one hundred citrons <note>original: *bījapura*</note> carried for a distance equal to the number of Suns <note>the number 12, representing the twelve solar months</note> in *krośas* <note>a measure of distance roughly 2 miles</note>, the intermediate fee is thirty-seven <note>original: *nagāgni*, where *naga* (mountains) is 7 and *agni* (fires) is 3, read right-to-left as 37</note>;
> Then if the distance traveled is only three *krośas*, O best of calculators, tell me quickly what is the fee to be paid? You shall be honored in the assembly of mathematicians!

---

<note>
The term "leaked" (skanna) here refers to material that has dripped or flowed out.
</note>

<note>
1. Here <gloss>Fee × Distance</gloss> / <gloss>Remaining Distance</gloss> = Sum
</note>

<note>
The equation: Fee (Sum - Goods) = (Sum - x) × x
</note>

This equation is derived according to the rule. In this context, the value of 'x' is stated by the teacher to be the desired intermediate transport fee.

<meta>This page continues the calculation from the previous page regarding an intermediate transport fee for a load of one hundred citrons. The previous page established the problem where a fee of 37 citrons was charged at the 3-mile mark of a 12-mile journey.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

**Statement of values:**

| 12 | 3 |
| :--- | :--- |
| 100 | 100 |
| 37 | 0 |

The number of citron fruits resulting from the intermediate transport fee is 10. 

**Calculation:**

Subtracting the 3 <term>yojanas; a measure of distance</term> already traveled from the standard distance of 12 *yojanas*, the remaining distance to be traveled is 9 *yojanas*. 

The quantity of goods (100) multiplied by the total distance (12) equals 1,200. This divided by the distance remaining (9) results in 400/3; this is called the "sum" <term>original: *yoga*</term>. 

From this "sum" of 400/3, subtract the initial goods (100); the remainder is 100/3. This multiplied by the given fee of 37 results in a "product" <term>original: *ghāta*</term> of 3,700/3. 

Thus, the "sum" and "product" are established as 400/3 and 3,700/3 respectively. Following the rule: "The square root of the square of the sum minus four times the product is the difference," the "difference" <term>original: *vivara*</term> is calculated as 340/3. 

By the process of concurrent division <term>original: *saṅkramaṇa*; an algebraic method to find two unknown numbers when their sum and difference are known</term>, the two resulting quantities are 370/3 and 10. Of these two, the lesser value must be taken as the answer.

### Rule for Truth and Falsehood

> The number of men, multiplied by a chosen number increased by one, 
> And then diminished by twice that chosen number, results in the Falsehoods.
> Subtracting those from the square of the number of men,
> The remainder tells the number of Truthful statements.

### Example

> There are five lovers of another's wife; 
> Two among them are dear to her, and three are not. 
> To each one of them, she says the same wonderful thing: "You are my dear one." 
> Tell me quickly, which of these statements are Truths and which are Falsehoods?
<note>This is a recreational math problem using a poetic scenario to practice a specific algebraic rule.</note>

Thus ends the section on **Mixed Practices** <term>original: *Miśra-vyavahāra*; a chapter dealing with various compound interest and proportion problems</term> in the *Gaṇitakaumudī* <note>The "Moonlight of Mathematics," written in 1356 CE</note> composed by **Nārāyaṇa Paṇḍita**, the son of Narasiṃha (who was a storehouse of all arts) and a Creator <note>literally "Four-faced," an epithet of the god Brahmā</note> in the science of mathematics.

# Now begins the Practice of Progressions.

### Rule

->The common difference multiplied by the number of terms minus one,<-
->added to the first term, is the last term. This last term,<-
->plus the first term and then halved, is the mean amount. That<-
->multiplied by the number of terms becomes the sum. || 1 ||<-
->Half of the number of terms minus one, multiplied by the common difference,<-
->added to the first term and multiplied by the number of terms, results in the sum.<-

### Example

->On the first day, a wealthy man gives to an excellent twice-born<-
->three *niṣkas* <term>a denomination of gold coin</term>; every day [he continues] with an increase of two...<-

---

1. **Here is the proof:** The proof of the first rule—"the common difference multiplied by the number of terms minus one, added to the first term"—is made clear by the method of proof provided by Bhāskara <note>referring to Bhāskara II, the famous 12th-century mathematician</note>. The proof for the first half of the second rule follows from the first rule as: 
Mean amount = $\frac{First + Difference \times (Terms - 1) + First}{2}$

$= First + \frac{Difference}{2} \times (Terms - 1).$ 
Therefore, the total sum = $Terms \times Mean\ Amount$

$= Terms \left\{ First + \frac{Difference}{2} \times (Terms - 1) \right\}$; by this, the logic is clear.

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on arithmetic progressions from the previous page, specifically calculating the final term, the mean term, and the total sum, before moving on to formulas for finding the first term and the common difference when the sum is known.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

->Tell me, friend, what are the last term, the mean term,<-
->and the total sum for half a month, if your calculation is correct. ||<-

**Statement of values:** First term <term>original: *ādi*; the starting value</term>: 3. Common difference <term>original: *uttara*; the amount added each day</term>: 2. Number of terms <term>original: *gaccha*; the duration in days</term>: 15. The resulting last term <term>original: *antyadhana*</term>: 31. Mean term <term>original: *madhyadhana*</term>: 17. Total sum <term>original: *sarvadhana*</term>: 255. This is what is designated as the "calculation" <note>the total sum of the arithmetic progression</note>.

# Example

->With a first term of [two] and a daily increase of three,<-
->tell me quickly, what are the mean, the last term, and the total sum<-
->earned by a certain merchant over six days?<-

**Statement of values:** First term: 2. Common difference: 3. Number of terms: 6. The resulting last term: 17.

The mean term: $\frac{19}{2}$ <note>9.5</note>. The sum: 57. In this case, because there is an even number of days <term>original: *sama-dina-gaccha*</term>, there is no single "middle day."

Therefore, it should be demonstrated to students that the mean term is half the sum of the amounts given on the two days immediately before and after the [theoretical] middle point.

# Rule for Finding the First Term

->The first term is the sum divided by the number of terms,<-
->minus half the product of the common difference and the number of terms minus one. || 2 ||<-

# Example

->A traveler went a distance of one hundred and sixty-eight *yojanas*<-
->over seven days, with a daily increase of three.<-
->Tell me, O scholar, what was the first term?<-
->Speak, if you have mastery over the wonders of progressions!<-

**Statement of values:** First term: 0 <note>The value to be found is initially set as zero in the layout</note>. Common difference <term>original: *caya*</term>: 3. Number of terms: 7. Sum of the progression <term>original: *śreḍhī-phala*</term>: 168. The first term is known to be: 15.

# Example

->With a first term of four, O scholar, the sum is<-
->two hundred and sixteen. Tell me, with what common difference<-
->was this achieved over nine days?<-

1. This rule—"The first term is the sum divided by the number of terms"—is consistent with the methods of Bhāskara <note>referring to Bhāskara II, the famous 12th-century mathematician</note>.

### Rule for Finding the Number of Terms

->Multiply the sum of the progression by twice the common difference,<-
->and add the square of the difference between the first term and half the common difference. || 3 ||<-
->Take the square root of this sum, subtract the first term,<-
->add half the common difference, and divide the result by the common difference to find the number of terms.<-

### Example

->Friend, the first term is eight and the common difference is six.<-
->The total sum of the calculation is three hundred less twelve <note>288</note>.<-
->Tell me quickly, what is the number of terms that produced this result?<-

Statement: First term: 8. Common difference: 6. Number of terms: 0 <note>unknown</note>. Sum of the progression: 288. The calculated number of terms <term>original: *gaccha*; the "going" or count of the series</term> is 6.

### Rule

->Assume a desired value for the number of terms,<-
->the first term, or the common difference. || 4 ||<-
->Whichever remains unknown should then be determined<-
->by applying the specific rules as previously described.<-

### Example

->The sum of a progression is one hundred, but the first term<-
->and the other values have been forgotten. If you are an expert<-
->in the methods of progressions, tell me the first term and the rest!<-

Statement: <term>First term</term>. <term>Common difference</term>. <term>Number of terms</term>. Sum of progression: 100. 

In this case, the first term and the number of terms are assumed arbitrarily: <term>First term: 1</term>. <term>Common difference: 0</term>. <term>Number of terms: 10</term>. Sum of progression: 100. By applying the rule for the common difference, the result is 2. 

Alternatively, if the common difference and the number of terms are assumed: <term>First term: 0</term>. <term>Common difference <term>original: *uttara*</term>: 1</term>. <term>Number of terms: 10</term>. Sum of progression: 100.

1. 'Multiply the sum of the series by twice the common difference...' This phrasing is exactly in accordance with the rules stated by Bhāskara <note>Bhāskara II, the 12th-century mathematician; this refers to his work, the *Līlāvatī*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on arithmetic progressions where multiple variables are unknown. Having previously defined rules for finding a single missing variable, the text now addresses complex problems where the first term, common difference, and the number of terms are all unknown, but their product and the total sum of the series are provided.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>

The first term is known through the previous rule for the first term as 11/2. <note>original: "jñāta ādisūtreṇādiḥ"; likely referring to a calculation from the preceding folio where an assumed value was tested.</note>

## Rule

->The product [of the three unknowns] is divided by an assumed first term;<-
->the result is called the "sequence-product" <term>original: *krama*; here representing the product of the common difference and the number of terms ($d \times n$)</term>.<-
->Twice the first term, added to the sequence-product and multiplied by the sequence-product, is then halved.<-
->That result, divided by the sum of the progression added to half the sequence-product, gives the common difference.<-
->The sequence-product divided by that common difference is the number of terms.<-

## Example

->Friend, the product of the first term, the number of terms, and the common difference is twelve.<-
->The sum of the progression is ten. Tell me,<-
->what is the measure of the first term, the number of terms, and the common difference?<-
->Or, [solve for a case where] the product is one and the sum is the same.<-

Statement: The product of the first term, common difference, and number of terms is 12. The sum of the progression is 10. By assuming one <note>as the first term</note>—

---

<margin>1</margin> Here is the derivation: When the product of the first term, common difference, and number of terms is divided by the assumed first term, the quotient is the product of the common difference and the number of terms, which is called the "sequence-product" <term>original: *krama*</term>. Now, let the common difference be represented by $d$. According to the previous method, the sum of the progression ($S$) is:
$S = \frac{n}{2} \{ 2a + d(n - 1) \}$
$S = \frac{dn}{2d} \{ 2a + dn - d \} = \frac{k}{2d} ( 2a + k - d )$ <note>where $k$ is the sequence-product ($dn$)</note>
Therefore, $2d \cdot S = 2a \cdot k + k^2 - d \cdot k$
By rearranging the terms: $d ( 2S + k ) = k ( 2a + k )$

$$d = \frac{k ( 2a + k )}{2S + k} = \frac{\frac{k ( 2a + k )}{2}}{S + \frac{k}{2}}$$

Furthermore, since $k = d \cdot n$, then $n = \frac{k}{d}$. Thus, the entire procedure is proved.

<note>
**Technical Vocabulary:**
*   **Rule** (Sūtram): A concise mathematical formula or theorem.
*   **Example** (Udāharaṇam): A practical problem to demonstrate the rule.
*   **Statement** (Nyāsaḥ): The formal setting down of known and unknown values.
*   **Derivation** (Upapattiḥ): The logical proof or step-by-step algebraic justification.
*   **Sum of the progression** (Śreḍhīphalam): The total value of all terms in the series.
*   **First term** (Ādi): The starting value of the series.
*   **Number of terms** (Gaccha): The count of terms in the series.
*   **Common difference** (Uttara / Caya): The constant amount by which each term increases.
*   **Product** (Ghāta): The result of multiplication.
</note>

<keywords>arithmetic progression, krama, common difference, algebra, derivation, Sanskrit mathematics, quadratic rearrangement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the resolution of the mathematical problems introduced on the previous page, where the first term, common difference, and number of terms are unknown. It demonstrates that by assuming different values for one variable, an infinite number of valid solutions can be found. It then transitions to a new rule concerning the equality of two different progressions.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

The resulting first terms, common differences, and number of terms are: <note>original: "jātā ādyuttaragacchāḥ"</note> 1/2 | 11/4 | 16/7.
By [assuming] two: 2/1 | 30/13 | 13/5.
By [assuming] three: 3/1 | 5/3 | 9/5.

In the second example, the product of the first term, common difference, and number of terms is 12. The sum of the progression is 1. <note>original: "gaṇitam 1"; here "gaṇita" refers to the total sum of the series.</note>
By [assuming] a half:
The resulting first terms, common differences, and number of terms are: 1/2 | 3/2 | 4/3.
By [assuming] a third: 1/3 | 11/5 | 15/11.
Thus, by the power of choice, there is an infinity [of possible solutions].

### Rule

->Where one [series] has a large first term and a small common difference,<-
->and the other has a small first term and a large common difference,<-
->the difference between their first terms, divided by the difference between<-
->half of their common differences, plus one, is the number of terms.<-
<note>This rule determines the specific number of terms ($n$) at which two different arithmetic progressions will yield the same total sum.</note>

<margin>1</margin> Here is the derivation: Let the first term of the first series be $a_1$, the common difference be $d_1$, and the number of terms be $n_1$. <note>original: "mu" for mukha (face/start), "ca" for caya (increase), "ga" for gaccha (number of terms).</note> Similarly, let the second series have first term $a_2$ and common difference $d_2$. According to the requirements of the problem, if the number of terms is $n$, then:

$$\frac{n}{2} \{ 2a_1 + d_1 ( n - 1 ) \} = \frac{n}{2} \{ 2a_2 + d_2 ( n - 1 ) \}$$

By simplification <term>original: *samaśodhana*; the algebraic process of removing like terms from both sides of an equation</term> and other steps:

$$2 ( a_1 - a_2 ) - d_1 + d_2 = n ( d_2 - d_1 )$$
$$\therefore n = \frac{2 ( a_1 - a_2 ) + d_2 - d_1}{d_2 - d_1} = \frac{2 ( a_1 - a_2 )}{d_2 - d_1} + 1$$
$$= \frac{a_1 - a_2}{\frac{d_2 - d_1}{2}} + 1 = \frac{a_1 - a_2}{\frac{d_2}{2} - \frac{d_1}{2}} + 1$$

Thus, the rule is proven. <note>original: "ityupapannam"</note>

In this case, it is clear that if the first term of the first series is greater than the second ($a_1 > a_2$), then the common difference of the second series must be greater than the first ($d_2 > d_1$) for the result to be a positive value; otherwise, it is not.

<vocab>gaccha: number of terms, mukha: first term, caya: common difference, upapatti: proof or derivation, sūtra: rule or formula, gaṇita: sum of the series, samaśodhana: algebraic simplification/balancing, dhanātmikā: positive value, labdhi: quotient or result</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of arithmetic progressions (series), specifically calculating when two different travelers—each increasing their speed daily at different rates—will have covered the same total distance.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>

### Example

->On the first day, nine <note>original: "nidhi" (treasure), a word-numeral standing for 9</note> leagues <note>original: "yojana," a unit of distance approximately 8-9 miles</note> are traveled<-
->And the traveler proceeds with a constant increase of five each day.<-
->Another starts with two leagues on the first day<-
->And increases by seven each day. Tell me, after how many days will they meet?<-

**Statement of the Problem**: 
| First Term: 9 | Increase: 5 | Terms: 0 | 
The resulting <term>gaccha</term> <note>The number of terms or days</note> is 8.
| First Term: 2 | Increase: 7 | Terms: 0 |
The meeting of the travelers occurs at 212 leagues.

### Rule

->The small common difference of the first series,<-
->a desired first term, and the number of terms for the second.<-
->The result <note>the total sum</note> of the first series<-
->is assumed to be the sum of the second series.<-
->The product of the number of terms and the common difference added to the first term<-
->become the starting values for the second meeting.<-

---

<margin>1</margin> Here is the derivation: Let the first term, common difference, and number of terms of the first series be $a_1$, $d_1$, and $n_1$ <note>original: "mu1" (mukha/face), "ca1" (caya/increase), "ga1" (gaccha/steps)</note>. Let the second series be $a_2$, $d_2$, and $n_2$. Then, for the total duration of $n_1 + n_2$, the sum <note>or "mathematical result"</note> of the first is:
$\frac{n_1 + n_2}{2} \{ 2a_1 + d_1 (n_1 + n_2 - 1) \}$

Similarly, for the duration $n_2$, the sum of the second series is:
$\frac{n_2}{2} \{ 2a_2 + d_2 (n_2 - 1) \}$. At the point of meeting, the distances covered by both are equal. Now, if $a_1, d_1, n_1, d_2, n_2$ are known values, then the first distance is also known. By assigning that same distance as the sum for the second series, the second "first term" can be easily found using the previous method from the known common difference and number of terms. Furthermore, the velocity on the first day for the first traveler = $a_1 + d_1 (n_1 + n_2)$. The velocity on the first day for the second traveler = $a_2 + d_2 \cdot n_2$. To calculate the second meeting point, these should be assumed as the new "first terms" and the value of the second meeting can be determined by the rule just stated.

<summary>This page provides a practical example and a general rule for determining when two people traveling at different accelerating speeds will have covered the same total distance. It includes a mathematical derivation explaining how to set the sums of two arithmetic progressions equal to solve for unknown variables.</summary>

<keywords>arithmetic progression, yojana, gaccha, mukha, caya, meeting point, series sum, kinematics</keywords>

### Example

> A man goes ahead for eight days with a certain first term and increase; 
> another follows with a certain first term and an increase of two, in order. 
> Tell me, friend, on which days did they meet twice on the path? 
> Speak, if you are a Lion-King <note>original: "harīndra," a title for a preeminent scholar</note> skilled in the battle-arena of the series-altar <note>original: "śreḍhī-vedī," the mathematical study of progressions</note> against the wild elephants of difficulty.

Here is the procedure. In this case, the first traveler’s first term is assumed to be two [2] and the common difference is four [4].

**Statement of the First Traveler:**
| First Term: 2 | Difference: 4 | Terms: 0 | 
The days spent going ahead: 8.

For the second traveler, the first term is assumed to be 0 and the difference is 2. The desired number of terms <note>the time elapsed for the second traveler</note> is assumed to be 4.

When the days spent going ahead are added to this, the first traveler's total terms become 12.

**Calculation:**
| First Term: 2 | Difference: 4 | Terms: 12 | 
The <term>gaṇita</term> <note>the sum or total distance covered</note> is 288.

This is the distance covered by the second traveler at the time of the first meeting. Now, as described before, the first term for the second traveler’s next stage is 66. The product of the number of terms and the difference, when added to the first term, is 50 for the first traveler and 77 for the second.

<insert>
These two are the assumed first terms:
| First Term: 50 | Difference: 4 | Terms: 0 |
By the rule "the difference of those first terms" and so on, there arises:
| First Term: 77 | Difference: 2 | Terms: 0 |
</insert>

The time of the second meeting, counting forward from the first meeting, is 28 days. When these are added separately to the previous terms [12 and 4], the total terms become 40 and 32 respectively. The <term>yojanas</term> <note>units of distance</note> covered by both travelers are equal at 3200.

Now, if the time of the second meeting is determined by calculation, then by subtracting from those two sets of terms, the remainder would be the time of the first meeting. For example:

In that same example, let the assumed number of terms for the second traveler be sixteen [16]. With the days spent going ahead added, the terms for the first traveler become 24.

Thus, for the first traveler:
| First Term: 2 | Difference: 4 | Terms: 24 | 
The distance [sum] is 1152.

Consequently, the first term for the second traveler is 57. Following the rule "the product of terms and difference with the first terms," the statement is:

<insert>
| First Term: 98 | Difference: 4 | Terms: 0 | 
Here, the characteristic of "one being larger or smaller" is not observed.
| First Term: 86 | Difference: 2 | Terms: 0 |
</insert>

When 98 is subtracted from 86 <note>likely implying the absolute difference in the context of the formula</note>, the remainder is 6. This is divided by the difference of the half-terms [1] and the result is 6. Because of the nature of the negative result <note>original: "ṛṇatvāt"</note>, adding one to the result yields 8 <note>the text suggests a specific algorithmic adjustment for finding the earlier meeting point</note>.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of arithmetic progressions from page 111, specifically calculating the number of days (gaccha) when two individuals with different movement patterns meet. It concludes one complex example and introduces a new rule for a meeting between a person moving at a constant speed and one moving at an accelerating speed.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>

When these are combined with the previous terms—24 and 16—the separate resulting terms produced are 16 and 8. Regarding the first meeting, the previous terms vary in many ways due to the nature of the second summation.

### Rule

-> **The constant speed, minus the first term,** <-
-> **divided by half the common difference and then increased by one, is the number of terms.** <-

### Example

> A lovesick woman, fondly remembering her lover, 
> travels every day at a constant rate of ten <term>yojanas</term> <note>a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly 8 to 13 kilometers</note>. 
> A procuress <note>original: "shambhali," a female messenger or go-between</note> followed her, 
> starting with three units and increasing her speed by two each day. 
> Tell me, on which day will their meeting take place?

**Statement:** 
The speed of the woman: 10. 
The speed of the procuress <margin>“Kuttini and Shambhali are synonymous,” according to the Amarakosha <note>a famous Sanskrit lexicon</note>.</margin>: 
First term (a): 3; Common difference (d): 2.

The resulting days of meeting: 8.

---

**1. Here is the mathematical proof <note>original: "upapatti"</note>:**
Let the constant speed of the first person = $s$ <note>original: "sa" for samagati</note>.
The initial value for the messenger = $u$ <note>original: "mu" for mukhya</note>.
The common difference = $c$ <note>original: "cha" for chaya</note>.
Let the day of meeting = $n$ <note>original: "ga" for gaccha</note>.

Then:
The distance traveled by the first person = $s \times n$.
The distance traveled by the messenger:
= $\frac{n}{2} \{ 2u + c(n - 1) \}$

These two distances are equal.
Therefore: $\frac{n}{2} \{ 2u + c(n - 1) \} = s \times n$

Or: $2u + c(n - 1) = 2s$ <note>dividing both sides by $n$ and multiplying by 2</note>

$\therefore n = \frac{2s - 2u}{c} + 1 = \frac{s - u}{\frac{c}{2}} + 1$

<term>niyatagati</term>: constant speed; 
<term>caya</term>: common difference; 
<term>shambhali / kuttini</term>: a procuress or female go-between; 
<term>Amara</term>: refers to Amarasimha, author of the Amarakosha dictionary.
</vocab>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific word problems regarding travelers meeting to a more abstract mathematical treatment of sequences. It provides a generalized rule for treating squares and cubes as arithmetic progressions, allowing the mathematician to apply the same summation formulas used in the previous examples to higher powers.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

### A General Rule for Summations, Squares, and Cubes

->**One, two, and one**<-
->**are the differences; for summations and squares, the start is one.**<-

---

1. In a summation <note>original: "sankalita," often referring to the sum of the first *n* natural numbers</note>, the starting value is one and the common difference <note>original: "chaya"</note> is to be considered one. For a square <note>original: "varga"</note>, the common difference is two. The starting value <note>original: "adi"</note> for both of these is simply one. 

For a cube <note>original: "ghana"</note>, the starting value is equal to the number of terms <note>original: "pada"</note>. The common difference should be considered double that starting value. In this way, whatever combination of these one desires to calculate, the starting value of the sum is the sum of the individual starting values, and the common difference of the sum is the sum of the individual differences.

**The logic behind this rule <note>original: "upapatti"</note>:**
Suppose we wish to find the summation, the square, and the cube of a number of terms *n* <note>original: "pa" for "pada"</note>.
Then:
Summation (*S*) = $1 + 2 + 3 + ... + n$. 
Therefore: Start (*a*) <note>original: "su" for "sukha/mukha"</note> = 1; Difference (*d*) <note>original: "cha" for "chaya"</note> = 1.

Square (*V*) = $n^2$ = $\frac{n}{2} (2n) = \frac{n}{2} \{2 + 2n - 2\}$
= $\frac{n}{2} \{1 \times 2 + 2 (n - 1)\}$
Thus, here: Start (*a*) = 1; Difference (*d*) = 2. <note>This demonstrates that the square of *n* is equal to the sum of an arithmetic progression of *n* terms starting at 1 with a difference of 2.</note>

Similarly, 
Cube (*G*) = $n^3 = \frac{n}{2} (2n^2) = \frac{n}{2} \{2n + 2n^2 - 2n\}$
= $\frac{n}{2} \{n \times 2 + 2n (n - 1)\}$
Thus, here: Start (*a*) = *n*; Difference (*d*) = 2*n*. <note>For example, $3^3 = 27$. Using this rule: terms = 3, start = 3, difference = 6. The sequence is 3, 9, 15. The sum is $3 + 9 + 15 = 27$.</note>

Furthermore:
Summation + Square = $\frac{n}{2} (n + 1) + \frac{n}{2} \{1 \times 2 + 2 (n - 1)\}$
= $\frac{n}{2} [ \{1 \times 2 + 1 (n - 1)\} + \{1 \times 2 + 2 (n - 1)\} ]$
= $\frac{n}{2} [ (1 + 1) \times 2 + (1 + 2) (n - 1) ]$
Therefore, in this combined case:
Start (*a*) = 1 + 1; Difference (*d*) = 1 + 2. 
It is proven that this logic can be applied everywhere.

<sig>15</sig>

Summation <note>sankalita</note>, Square <note>varga</note>, Cube <note>ghana</note>, General Rule <note>upalakshana-sutra</note>, Common Difference <note>pracaya/chaya</note>, Starting Term <note>adi/mukha</note>, Proof/Logic <note>upapatti</note>, Term/Number of terms <note>pada</note>.

<summary>This page explains how to represent squares and cubes as arithmetic progressions. It provides specific starting values and common differences for each, demonstrating that complex mathematical powers can be solved using simpler summation techniques.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic progression, squares, cubes, summation, mathematical proof, Sanskrit mathematics, algebra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 113 regarding how higher powers—specifically squares and cubes—can be treated as arithmetic progressions. It provides specific values for the "starting term" and "common difference" when calculating a cube, and then moves into a complex example that combines these different types of sums.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

->Regarding the cube, the form is equal to the number of terms;<-
->the starting value and common difference are doubled.<-
->By adding the differences, three types are combined;<-
->the sum of those starting values is the combined starting value.<-

# Example

> Tell me separately, my friend, the summation, the square, and the cube of five.
> Then, find the sum of any two and the sum of all three using the **<term>series</term><note>original: "sredhi"; in this context, it refers to the mathematical rules governing arithmetic progressions</note>** method, and do it quickly!

**Set-up:**
For the summation: Start (*a*) = 1, Difference (*d*) = 1, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
For the square: Start (*a*) = 1, Difference (*d*) = 2, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
For the cube: Start (*a*) = 5, Difference (*d*) = 10, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
The results are: Summation = 15, Square = 25, Cube = 125.

For the sum of the summation and the square: Start (*a*) = 2, Difference (*d*) = 3, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
For the sum of the summation and the cube: Start (*a*) = 6, Difference (*d*) = 11, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
For the sum of the square and the cube: Start (*a*) = 6, Difference (*d*) = 12, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
For the sum of all three (summation + square + cube): Start (*a*) = 7, Difference (*d*) = 13, Terms (*n*) = 5. 
The results are 40, 140, 150, and 165 respectively. This is demonstrated through the **<term>series</term>** method to show the beauty of this mathematical operation.

# Rule

->Half the number of terms multiplied by the number of terms plus one<-
->is known as the "summation."<-
->Three times that first value, minus the number of terms,<-
->is equal to the sum of the square and the summation.<-

In the previous example, to find the sum of the square and the summation:
Number of terms (*n*) = 5. The summation is 15. The sum of the square and the summation is 40.

---

1. **<term>The Logic/Proof</term><note>original: "upapatti"; this section provides the mathematical reasoning or derivation for the rules stated above</note>**: The summation is exactly as described by the teacher Bhaskara.

The sum of the square and the summation $= n^2 + \frac{n}{2} (n + 1) = \frac{n}{2} \{ 2n + n + 1 \}$

$= \frac{n}{2} (3n + 3 - 2) = 3 \cdot \frac{n}{2} (n + 1) - n$. Thus, the rule is proved.

<vocab>sankalita (summation), sredhi (series), nyasa (set-up/layout), upapatti (proof/logic), Bhaskara (famous 12th-century mathematician), kriti (square), ghana (cube), pada (number of terms)</vocab>

### Rule

->Take the sum of the **<term>summation</term><note>The "summation" (saṅkalita) refers to the sum of the first $n$ integers: $n(n+1)/2$.</note>** and the square,<-
->multiply it by six times the number of terms plus one;<-
->add four times the number of terms to this,<-
->and divide by nine to find the sum of the summation and the cube.<-

Therefore, Summation + Terms³ = —————— ( Terms + 1 ) + Terms³
           2
